To recapitulate, Cheung and Mangan [90]’s construction can be taken as
providing thedefinitionof EoM level manifest CK duality:
A classical field theory exhibitsEoM level manifest CK duality
if its EoM can be reformulated as BAS equations by a field
redefinition that is invertible on asymptotic scattering states.
To show that this EoM level manifest CK duality implies the manifest CK du-
ality of scattering amplitudes, Cheung and Mangan [90] utilize the framework
of BG recursion [154] (perturbiner method [155–157]). Details are to be found
in Ref. [90], but nevertheless a quick review is provided in Sec. 4.A.
Note also that it is unnecessary to invoke Lagrangian formulations in this
discussion, although the Freedman-Townsend [539] formulation of NLSM may
provide tempting pathways: see Sec. 4.B.

Sec. 3.4.3. For the reader’s sake, let us reproduce their equations below up to
minor change in notation.

For NLSM, the CKR mapped a global Lie algebra index (flavor index) to a
kinematic index. For BI theory, the CKR maps the gauged index (color index)
of YM theory to a kinematic index. As a result, one inevitably encounters
the notion of “diffeomorphism gauge transformation” and “diffeomorphism
gauge covariance,” which seem nonsensical at first sight. What does it mean
to take diffeomorphisms as the gauge algebra of a YM theory? What does
“YMYM(sdiff(M))” mean? Crucially, the EoM level CK duality strictly necessi-
tates such concepts, as is asserted by Cheung and Mangan [90] and as should
be clear from our review and exposition above. To our understanding, however,
this point has not been properly clarified nor addressed in the literature.
With this remark made, we proceed to Secs. 4.3 and 4.4 to define diffeomor-
phism gauge theory as a strict implication of EoM level CK duality.
4.3 Ordinary Gauge Theory
To begin with, we review basic concepts in ordinary gauge theory. By an
ordinary gauge theory, we mean a gauge theory whose gauge group is a finite-
dimensional matrix Lie groupG. For concreteness, we may takeG= SU(N).
The gauge algebrag=su(N) describes fundamental indicesi,j,k,l,···and
adjoint indicesa,b,c,d,···. Forψ∈C N andu,v∈su(N), we denote

4.3.1 Gauge Covariance
In ordinary gauge theories such as YM theory, local operators are subject to
localG-transformations, geometrically formulated as transformations acting
on thefibersof a bundle over spacetime. In this sense, gauge transformations
are said to beinternaltransformations: the points in the base spacetime
manifold are not altered at all. It is important to understand that gauge
transformations define equivalences, or redundancies, under a gauge orbit, so
physically meaningful quantities are gauge-invariant objects.
To describe gauge transformations in an explicit manner, letg(x) assign a
group element inGfor each spacetime pointx. Gauge-covariant operators,
such as those valued in the singlet, fundamental, or adjoint, exhibit the fol-
lowing transformation behaviors:
Singlet:ϕ(x)7→ϕ(x),
Fund.:ϕ i(x)7→g ij(x)ϕj(x),
Adjoint:ϕ a(x)7→g ab(x)ϕb(x).
(4.22)
Being valued in the singlet is a synonym for being gauge invariant.
Suppose a non-singlet field. Due to the fact that the parameterg(x) vary
over spacetime, the field values at different points transform differently. As a
result, their difference is not physically meaningful. The implication is that
the partial derivative of a non-singlet field is not a gauge-covariant object.
This provides the very motivation for introducing the gauge connectionA,
which defines the gauge-covariant derivativeD. For each gauge-covariant op-
erator, the covariant derivative returns an operator with one more spacetime
index that exhibits thesamegauge-covariant transformation behavior:
Singlet:D αϕ(x) =∂ αϕ(x),

4.3.2 Wilson Line
Geometrically, the gauge connectionAdefines parallel transport along con-
tours in spacetime. For a contourCthat stretches from pointPto pointP ′,
the parallel transport establishes an isomorphism between the gauge fibers at
PandP ′. This isomorphism describes a group element inGassigned for the
contourC, which is the Wilson lineW C.
To be concrete, suppose a finite contourCparameterized ass7→x µ +yµ(s),
wherey µ(0) = 0 ands∈[0,1]. The pointPhas coordinatesx µ, while the
pointP ′ has coordinatesx µ +y µ(1). The parallel transport equation for this
contour is given by

In Eq. (4.32), Ψi(0) and Ψi(1) are covariant objects located at the pointsP
andP′ (xµ andx µ+yµ(1)), respectively. In particular, they transform with the
gauge parameters atPandP ′. This establishes that the gauge transformation
behavior of the Wilson line is
WC 7→g(P ′)WCg−1(P).(4.33)
Namely, the Wilson line transforms bilocally.
WhenCis a loop, a singlet is formed by taking a trace ofW C for a chosen
representation. This singlet is the Wilsonloop, which should be distinguished
from the open Wilsonlinedescribed above. The former is a number, whereas
the latter is a matrix (group element). The focus of this chapter is put on
Wilson lines rather than Wilson loops.
An implication of the bilocal behavior in Eq. (4.33) is that a gauge-covariant

4.3.3 Covariant Taylor Expansion
In Eqs. (4.34) and (4.35), we have seen that the point of covariantization can be
shifted by attaching Wilson lines. For instance,W Cϕ(P) in Eq. (4.34), which
defines a nonlocal operator, transforms like an operator at the pointP ′. In
this light, one might wonder if it is possible to explicitly expressW Cϕ(P) as
an infinite sum of local operators atP ′.
This is indeed possible, assuming thatP ′ is sufficiently close toP. To
understand this explicitly, let us consider a straight-line contours7→x µ +qµs,
whereq µ describes a fixed vector. The Wilson line for a part of this contour

The identity in Eq. (4.42) could be referred to as the covariant Taylor ex-

from the Wilson lines.
4.4 Diffeomorphism Gauge Theory via Color-Kinematics Duality
In this section, we take the diffeomorphism algebra in the sense that is implied
by CK duality. The strategy here is to construct exact counterparts of the well-
established constructions of ordinary gauge theory in Sec. 4.3 by mechanically
implementing the CKR:
g=su(N)−→g=diff(R d).(4.44)
This systematically deduces and defines what a diffeomorphism gauge theory
is. For instance, every Lie bracket ofsu(N) will be replaced with the Lie
bracket between vector fields.
To elaborate, the diffeomorphism Lie algebra inddimensions,diff(R d), is
identified with the space Γ(TR d) of vector fields onR d. For a scalar field
ψ∈C ∞(Rd) and vector fieldsu,v∈diff(R d), we denote
uψ(x) =u µ(x)∂µψ(x),
[u(x),v(x)] µ =u ν(x)∂νvµ(x)−v ν(x)∂νuµ(x),
(4.45)
which defines the fundamental and adjoint actions in parallel with Eq. (4.21).
Note that the Lie bracket between vector fields, [u(x),v(x)] µ, is literally de-
rived by taking the commutator betweenu µ(x)∂µ andv µ(x)∂µ asdifferential
operators. The mathematical basis of this view is elaborated in Sec. 4.4.4. The
diffeomorphism Lie group is denoted asDiff(R d).
4.4.1 Gauge Covariance
In gravitational theories such as general relativity, there exists the freedom
to choose coordinates in spacetime. This introduces the notion ofgeneral
covariance, which describes how local fields change under coordinate trans-
formations. It is important to understand that coordinate transformations
in generally covariant theories define equivalencies, or redundancies, under a
gauge orbit, on which physically meaningful quantities shall be invariant.

===== PAGE 166 =====
144
However, at the technical, precise mathematical level, the gauge transforma-
tions for general covariance work differently than the gauge transformations
described in Sec. 4.3, i.e., those in the YM theory, for instance. Crucially, dif-
feomorphisms alter points in thebasespacetime manifold themselves. Namely,
they areexternaltransformations, not internal.
Despite this crucial difference, however, we push the idea of having the
diffeomorphism algebra as a gauge algebra in the YM sense, as a necessary
implication of CK duality.
We start with the transformation behavior of fields. Based on Eq. (4.25),
the CKR in Eq. (4.44) stipulates that the singlet, fundamental, and adjoint
transformation behaviors are defined as
Singlet:ϕ(x)7→ϕ(x),
Fund.:ϕ(x)7→ϕ(x) +ξ µ(x)∂µϕ(x),
Adjoint:ϕ µ(x)7→ϕ µ(x) +ξν(x)∂νϕµ(x)−ϕ ν(x)∂νξµ(x),
(4.46)
provided the fundamental and adjoint actions in Eq. (4.45). Here,ξ=ξ µ(x)∂µ∈
diff(Rd) is a diffeomorphism Lie algebra element serving as an infinitesimal
diffeomorphism gauge transformation parameter, which is the counterpart of
ε(x)∈su(N) in Eq. (4.25).
Suppose a non-singlet field, such as the fundamental field in Eq. (4.46). Due
to the fact that the diffeomorphism parameterξ µ(x) vary over spacetime,
the field values at different points transform differently. As a result, their
difference is not meaningful. The implication is that the partial derivative
of a non-singlet field is not a gauge-covariant object. Explicitly, the partial
derivative of the fundamental field,∂ αϕ(x), transforms to
∂α

ϕ(x) +ξµ(x)∂µϕ(x)

=∂ αϕ(x) +ξµ(x)∂α∂µϕ(x) +∂αξµ(x)∂µϕ(x),
(4.47)
the behavior of which is neither singlet, fundamental, or adjoint according to
Eq. (4.46).
In this light, we are invited to the idea of introducing a diffeomorphism
gauge connectionA, defining the diffeomorphism-covariant derivativeD. To

===== PAGE 167 =====
145
this end, one mechanically applies the CKR rule. From Eq. (4.23), one finds
Singlet:D αϕ(x) =∂ αϕ(x),
Fund.:D αϕ(x) =∂ αϕ(x) +Aµα(x)∂µϕ(x),
Adjoint:D αϕµ(x) =∂ αϕµ(x) +Aνα(x)∂νϕµ(x)−ϕ ν(x)∂νAµα(x),
(4.48)
while Eq. (4.26) stipulates that the diffeomorphism gauge connection trans-
forms as

α(x)−D αξµ(x).(4.49)
Recall the description given around Eq. (4.23). Again, for each diffeomorphism-
covariant operator, the diffeomorphism-covariant derivative returns an oper-
ator with one more spacetime index that exhibits thesamediffeomorphism-
covariant transformation behavior. That is, the additional spacetime index is
diffeomorphism-neutral.
Let us verify this claim in a direct, explicit fashion. For the singlet, Eq. (4.48)
stipulates that its covariant derivativeD αϕ(x) is simply the partial derivative
∂αϕ(x). By Eq. (4.46), this transforms as∂ αϕ(x)7→∂ αϕ(x): it is invariant.
Therefore,D αϕ(x) is still a singlet, where the indexαis neutral under the
diffeomorphism gauge transformations.

These calculations explicitly establish how the CKR rule concretely real-

4.4.4 A Mathematical Perspective
In the above exposition, we regarded vector fields as linear differential op-
erators. In pursuit of this differential operator approach, we computed and
definedW C as a differential operator and then identified it rather directly as
an element of the diffeomorphism group asW C∈Diff(R d), although a diffeo-
morphism onR d is usually thought of as a mapR d→Rd.
We shall remark that this identification is based on the differential operator
representation of diffeomorphisms [541] by the pullback, which traces back to
an algebraic perspective on geometry [541–547]. Below, we elaborate on this
mathematical viewpoint briefly.
The idea is that diffeomorphisms can be thought of as maps that preserve the
algebraic structure of smooth functions under pointwise addition and multipli-
cation: automorphisms of the commutative ringC ∞(Rd) of smooth functions.
Namely, a differential operatorU:C ∞(Rd)→C ∞(Rd) is a diffeomorphism
U∈Diff(R d) iff
U[ψ 1 +ψ 2 ] =U[ψ 1 ] +U[ψ 2 ], U[ψ 1ψ2 ] =U[ψ 1 ]U[ψ 2 ] (4.77)
for allψ 1,ψ 2∈C ∞(Rd). In this precise mathematical sense, the differential
operator in Eq. (4.64) has defined a diffeomorphismW C∈Diff(R d).
Similarly, vector fields onR d, as infinitesimal diffeomorphisms, are deriva-
tions on the commutative algebraC∞(Rd): they are differential operators that
preserve the pointwise addition and multiplication. That is, a differential op-
eratorv:C ∞(Rd)→C ∞(Rd) is a diffeomorphism generatorv∈diff(R d) iff
v[ψ 1 +ψ 2 ] =v[ψ 1 ] +v[ψ 2 ], v[ψ 1ψ2 ] =v[ψ 1 ]ψ 2 +ψ 1v[ψ 2 ].(4.78)
This definition implies thatvis a linear differential operator,v=v µ∂µ. The
Lie bracket between vector fields is simply the commutator between linear
differential operators.
In ordinary gauge theory, the gauge connection provides the isomorphism
between color spaces (finite-dimensional fibers) at two different points. Impor-

===== PAGE 175 =====
153
tantly, this law of parallel transport preserves the color Lie algebra structure.
In diffeomorphism gauge theory, the diffeomorphism gauge connection may
be thought of as providing the isomorphism between the infinite-dimensional
algebraC ∞(Rd) of smooth functions at two different points.
These mathematical formalizations also conform to those of noncommuta-
tive and Poisson gauge theories [542–550]. For instance, the diffeomorphism
Wilson line in Eq. (4.64) can be deduced as a generalization of the Poisson
Wilson line from, e.g., Refs. [551, 552].
These mathematical justifications, however, may not be conceived as pro-
viding the most clear or practically useful picture for physicists. Notably, a
simple geometrical interpretation is found below.
4.5 Interpretation as Teleparallel Theory
4.5.1 Frame and Anholonomy
As we have discussed in Sec. 2.3, ateleparallel theoryrefers to a field theory
of a dynamical frame field [296–307]. Strictly speaking, the name “teleparal-
lel theory” suits theories that describe geometries of absolute parallelism in
which the frame (diffeomorphism neutral) index isglobal, as is discussed above
Eq. (2.81). In the case of GR, we have seen in Eq. (2.73) that the frame index
is, in fact,gaugeddue to local Lorentz symmetry. Thus, the frame formula-
tion of GR in Eqs. (2.69) or (2.77) is not a teleparallel field theory in the strict
sense.
Agenuine teleparallel theoryis a field theory of a dynamical frameE µα
whose diffeomorphism neutral indexαis global, i.e., not charged under any
local symmetries. It governs a dynamical geometry of absolute parallelism.
Let us study a genuine teleparallel theory. For clarity, we useα,β,γ,δ,···
for the global diffeomorphism neutral index (sinceA,B,C,D,···have been
used and reserved as the local Lorentz index in our discussions on GR). With
this clarification in mind, we recall the elements of frame geometry from
Sec. 2.3. The frame’s degree of anholonomy, i.e., noncoordinateness, is cap-
tured by the Lie bracket [E α,Eβ], which we propose to denote asF αβ:

In conclusion, we realize that Cheung and Mangan [90]’s formulation of BI
theory, reproduced in Eq. (4.20), admits a remarkable geometric interpretation.
The diffeomorphism gauge connection introduced in Eq. (4.20a) is the vielbein
perturbation around the Kronecker vacuum,Aµα =E µα−δµα. The diffeomor-
phism field strength in Eq. (4.20b) admits a background-independent rewriting
as the anholonomyF µαβ = [Eα,Eβ]µ. The diffeomorphism YM equations in
Eq. (4.20c) admit a background-independent presentation asηγα[Eγ,Fαβ] = 0.
The diffeomorphism covariant derivative means the Lie derivative:Dα =£ Eα.
A few remarks are in order. First and foremost, not all diffeomorphism-
invariant theories of the dynamical vielbeinEwould turn out to be obtain-
able via applying a CKR on ordinary gauge theories. That is, a teleparallel
theory is not guaranteed to be a diffeomorphism gauge theory of the strict
sense. This point is best demonstrated by examples; we do so in Secs. 4.5.3
to 4.5.6. While the equations that we have explored just now are certainly
obtainable from the CKR, there are also objects in teleparallel geometry that
do not straightforwardly admit their counterparts in ordinary gauge theory.
For example, take the anholonomycoefficientsΩ αβγ, which involve the inverse
eαµ =δ αµ−δ ανAνβδβµ +O(A 2). For another example, take the determinant
|E|= 1+δ αµAµα +O(A 2) in general dimensions. The appearance of bare con-
nectionAmay cause problems when these objects are used in the Lagrangian,
for instance.
Secondly, it is known that teleparallel geometry could admit an analogy
with lattice systems at the level of mathematical frameworks [460, 516–520],

===== PAGE 178 =====
156
such as crystals with impurities. In this case one can import intuitions and
ideas such as Burgers vector and dislocation defects, as will be briefly touched
upon in Sec. 4.6. In this context a diffeomorphism-invariant dressing may be
possible, in connection with the diffeomorphism Wilson line in Sec. 4.4.2. The
rough idea is to flow along the lattice frame to find an anchor on the infinities.
While typical scalar fields are diffeomorphism fundamental, a dressed field
will be diffeomorphism singlet, exhibiting the invariant behavior envisioned in
Eq. (4.46).
Thirdly, it should be noted that our diffeomorphism gauge theory program
for the double copy seeks to represent an approach different from previous con-
structions such as Refs. [50, 553]’s bundle construction or double field theory
[554–560] (cf. generalized geometry [561–564]). In particular, our approach
does not employ any auxiliary or extra space other than the spacetime man-
ifoldM. In this picture, there is no such thing as two different species of
coordinatesx µ, ˜x˜µ. Ref. [90] writes down “A ˜µµ” as a notation, but both in-
dices there describe the same spacetime kind of indices.

4.5.4 Case Study II: Diffeomorphism Chern-Simons Theory
Another concrete instance of a diffeomorphism gauge theory is CS theory with
its color algebra replaced with the volume-preserving diffeomorphism algebra
in three dimensions,sdiff(M 3):
CKR : CSCS(g)7→ CSCS(sdiff(M3)). (4.102)
The double copy of CS theory was investigated in Ref. [533] with a construction
of a Lagrangian. A notable clarification has been given by a recent work [534]
at the Lagrangian level, showing that the double copy of CS theory is on-shell
equivalent to three-dimensional Einstein gravity:
CSCS(sdiff(M3)) ∼= GRGR3,(4.103)
Here, we would like to briefly illuminate a key aspect of Ref. [534]’s construc-
tion from our perspective, without reviewing every detail.
Ordinary CS theory is defined by the action that integrates the CS three-
form on a three-manifoldM 3, written in Eq. (2.116). Crucially, the CS three-
form requires the Killing formδ ab on the gauge algebrag.
In Sec. 4.5.3, we have seen that diffeomorphismBFtheory admits a La-
grangian formulation, facilitating the double copy at both EoM and Lagrangian
levels. As is well-known,BFand CS Lagrangians are closely related; the for-
mer provides the “cotangent” description of the latter. Crucially, however, the
CS Lagrangian requires the Killing formδ ab unlike theBFLagrangian. This
raises an immediate problem for the Lagrangian-level double copy: one has to
find a diffeomorphism-invariant Killing form.
Interestingly, Ref. [534] shows that a Killing form exists for the volume-
preserving diffeomorphism algebra in three dimensions, which is yet nonlocal.

===== PAGE 182 =====
160
The authors identify an expression that can be compared to the CS three-form
with the aid of a nonlocal operator. They then derive the Euler-Lagrangian
equations from their proposed CSCS(sdiff(M3)) action and findsF µαβ[A] = 0,
the flatness condition on the diffeomorphism gauge connection. By KCR, this
corresponds toF aαβ[A] = 0, the EoM of CS theory.
4.5.5 Case Study III: Diffeomorphism Yang-Mills Theory
Diffeomorphism YM theory can be defined as a classical teleparallel field the-
ory around the Kronecker vacuum whose EoM areη γα [Eγ,[Eα,Eβ]] = 0, as
discussed several times throughout our discussion. Note that this theory re-
quires the flat frame-indexed metricη γα as a background structure.
It seems unclear whether diffeomorphism YM theory could admit a La-
grangian formulation. It will be interesting to see if Ref. [534]’s Killing form
can be utilized. As a related subject, it could also be interesting to think
about the Pontryagin theory in four dimensions, (F∧F).
Ref. [90] established that the volume-preserving diffeomorphism YM equa-
tions provide a reformulation of BI theory. Here, we realized that they can be
written in terms of theE-variable asη γα [Eγ,[Eα,Eβ]]µ = 0 with∂ µEµα = 0,
yet with the crucial clarification that the Kronecker backgroundδ µα has to
be imposed. Hence it can be said that BI theory, at the classical EoM level,
admits a formulation as a teleparallel theory.
A physical lesson that can be elicited from this geometric rewriting is that
expanding around any background of the form ¯Eµα(x) =X µ,ν(x)δνα will also
yield the same BI scattering amplitudes, ifx µ7→X µ(x) gives a well-behaved
diffeomorphism that is trivialized at the infinities at a sufficiently fast speed.
This certainly changes many formulae (e.g., the diffeomorphism field strength)
and the EoM because the background ¯Eµα(x) is position-dependent. Verifying
if the same tree amplitudes are obtained as in the constant Kronecker vacuum
provides a nontrivial check and test of the teleparallel formulation of BI theory.
4.5.6 Case Study IV: Frame formulation of General Relativity
Diffeomorphism YM theories are genuine teleparallel theories, and there is
no local symmetry on the frame index. In contrast, the so-called teleparallel
equivalent of GR [304–307] is not a genuine teleparallel theory due to the local
Lorentz symmetry. This point applies to both action and EoM level formu-
lations; recall the discussion on the Mason-Newman [309] idea in Eq. (2.60).

===== PAGE 183 =====
161
An interesting perspective arises by the first-order action in Eq. (2.69), which
understands GR as a perturbation away from diffeomorphismBFtheory (sup-
pose a rescalingB7→κ 2B). Local Lorentz symmetry is not enjoyed in the
topological limit but emerges in the full theory.
Can the frame formulation of GR still be viewed as a diffeomorphism gauge
theory, which is not necessarily YM? Unfortunately, neither of the actions in
Eqs. (2.69) and (2.77) seems to allow for a KCR. The anholonomy coefficients
ΩABC involves the coframee Aµ inside which bare connection coefficientsA µA
are inserted. The dynamical combinationG µν
ABCD [E] in Eq. (2.69) also suffers
from this bare connection problem around the Kronecker vacuum. Hence, in
general dimensions, formulating GR as a genuine diffeomorphism gauge theory
still seems to be an open question.
4.5.7 General Relativity as a Diffeomorphism Gauge Theory
From the above case study, a lesson emerges that there exist different classes
of “teleparallel theories.”
• Teleparallel theory: A field theory of a dynamical frame.
• Genuine teleparallel theory: A field theory of a dynamical frame whose
diffeomorphism-neutral index is not subject to any local symmetry. Gov-
erns geometries of absolute parallelism.
• Nominal teleparallel theory: A teleparallel theory that is not genuine.
• Diffeomorphism gauge theory: A diffeomorphism-invariant teleparallel
theory that is mapped to an ordinary gauge theory by a choice of a KCR
rule when expanded around a choice of a vacuum.
According to this taxonomy, the theories in Secs. 4.5.3 to 4.5.5 describe diffeo-
morphism gauge theories at the EoM or Lagrangian levels, while the telepar-
allel equivalent of GR is a nominal teleparallel theory that seems to be also
not diffeomorphism gauge theory in general dimensions. It shall be remarked
that, although explorations on teleparallel gravity may generally turn out to
be fruitful for envisioning a manifest double copy formulation of GR or un-
derstanding CK duality, emphasis must be put on the viability of KCR as a
crucial criterion, eventually.
A few instances have been reported in three and four dimensions, however,
such that GR can be exactly reformulated as a diffeomorphism gauge theory
in the precise sense (classical level, on-shell equivalence).

===== PAGE 184 =====
162
• As noted earlier in Sec. 4.5.4, three-dimensional GR is shown to be
on-shell equivalent to volume-preserving Chern-Simons theory: GRGR3∼=
CSCS(sdiff(M3)). This refers to the result of Ref. [534], which works in
terms of Lagrangians.
• In four dimensions, the SD sector of GR is known to admit a formula-
tion as a volume-preserving SDYM theory at the EoM level: SDGRSDGR∼=
SDYMSDYM(sdiff(M4)). This refers to the result of Mason and Newman [309],
reviewed in Sec. 2.4.3.8 In this case the diffeomorphism gauge connection
is the perturbation of arescaledtetradVaround the Kronecker vacuum.
The three-dimensional GR construction of Ref. [534] depends on several cru-
cial features of three dimensions: first, the nonlocal Killing form construction,
second, an interesting identity that rewrites the quadratic part of the deter-
minant|E|via a nonlocal operator, third, the cubic nature of|E|around the
Kronecker background, and fourth, the spin connection can be killed in the
dreibein-Palatini formalism giving rise to a genuinely teleparallel description,
owing to the celebrated fact that Einstein gravity describes flat Riemannian
geometry on shell [565, 566]. It will be interesting to investigate if these struc-
tures could be properly generalized ind>3 and especially ind= 4. 9
Finally, it is interesting to note that the LL formulation of gravity, reviewed
in Sec. 2.1.2, points to suggestive formulae in the context of the current discus-
sion.10 Suppose the de Donder gauge condition in Eq. (2.17),∂ µgµα = 0, and

4.6 Misner String as a Diffeomorphism Vortex
So far, we have been studying diffeomorphism gauge theories as classical field
theories, mainly at the level of EoM. To be comprehensive, we wish to de-
scribeclassical solutionsof diffeomorphism gauge theories to some explicit
degree. Generally speaking, different classical solutions will arise as each the-
ory exhibits unique dynamics. For instance, the on-shell configurations of the
diffeomorphismBFtheory in Sec. 4.5.3 areholonomicframes, i.e., coordinate
vector fields. In contrast, the on-shell configurations of the diffeomorphism YM
theory in Sec. 4.5.5 are not necessarily holonomic: the anholonomy dynami-
cally evolves as the field strength. There are propagating degrees of freedom,
and the dynamics is far more complex.
The subject of finding and studying classical solutions of diffeomorphism
gauge theories seems to be an interesting program. It will be exciting if illu-
minations can be drawn on the classical double copy [160, 181–185] program,
either in general dimensions, in three-dimensions, or for SD gravity. The clas-
sical double copy program investigates correspondences and maps between
classical solutions of theories in the double-copy web in Fig. 4.2. In particular,
the KS classical double copy [160] has been mapping stationary solutions in GR
toMaxwelltheory solutions, which are then trivially embedded to YM theory
via a constant color factor. Naively, one could think of pursuing the same to
embed Maxwell theory solutions into a diffeomorphism YM theory, thinking
of a “constant” diffeomorphism generator. However, this can be subtle be-
cause the diffeomorphism algebra involves derivatives. It will be interesting if
this nontriviality can lead to insightful illuminations on the nature of classical
double copy.
In this work, we do not intend to carry out a comprehensive investigation
into classical solutions. Yet, we provide a simple yet concrete example that
defines a classical solution inanydiffeomorphism gauge theories: the Misner

===== PAGE 186 =====
164
string [474–478], reinterpreted in the teleparallel viewpoint.
As we have remarked in Sec. 3.5, the Misner string has been known as the
gravitational analog of the Dirac string, in which case the NUT charge is seen
as the gravitomagnetic charge [331, 479]. Notably, the teleparallel geometry
interpretation we have established in this chapter facilitates embedding the
Misner string to diffeomorphism gauge theories. As a result, we view the
Misner string as adiffeomorphism vortex, which exists in any diffeomorphism
gauge theories as a topological configuration. Surely, the adequate mathemat-
ical framework ismultivalueddiffeomorphism gauge transformation, which we
have established in detail in Sec. 3.4.

To clarify, we now liberate the Misner string from the GR context and view
Eqs. (4.115) and (4.116) as describing a classical solution in a teleparallel or

topological configuration generated by a multivaluedgaugetransformation, it
is (locally) on-shell in any theory regardless of the dynamics specified.
Consider the abelian Dirac string in Eq. (3.32). Its trivial embedding in
YM theory is given in Eq. (3.34), which uses a constant color factorλ a. This
constant color factor trivializes the nonabelian part of the field strengthF a,
which happens to be a common (and even characteristic) situation in classical
double copy [160]. Interestingly, the very same kind of trivialization hap-
pens in Eq. (4.117), which arises by applying the replacementλ a7→−nδ µt
on Eq. (3.35). Although the meaning of “constant diffeomorphism generator”
would be unclear in general, it appears that this effective constancy is achieved
by a collaboration between the direction of the referenceδµt and the orientation
of the surface support inducing monodromy for contours on thex-yplane.
With these understandings, we may identify the Misner string in Eq. (4.116),
as a classical solution in any diffeomorphism gauge theory, as the classical dou-
ble copy of the colored Dirac string in Eq. (3.34). In particular, this identifies
that the colored Dirac string in YM theory classically double copies to the
Misner string in the Cheung and Mangan [90]’s formulation of BI theory.
4.6.2 Mathematician’s Approach
Recalling Sec. 3.4.2, we also briefly remark on a description of the above Misner
string geometry that avoids using the Dirac delta. In this case, the surfaceS
will be excised from the spacetimeM, so one studies the manifoldM\S.
To begin with, the multivalued diffeomorphism may be formalized in the
basic framework of transition functions. Recall that, in manifold theory, a
transition function relates the coordinates of one chart to the coordinates
of another. Let (U i,φi) and (U j,φj) be two overlapping charts, whereφ i :
Ui →R d andφ j :U j →R d. The transition function describes the map
τij =φ i◦φ−1
j :φ j(Ui∩U j)→φ i(Ui∩U j).

inM. Suppose an open cover{U i}ofM\S. For each patchU i, we define

We end with a brief physical discussion on nonlocal functionals in the telepar-

In sum, we have established the Misner string as a classical solution in
any teleparallel or diffeomorphism gauge theory, which arises by applying a
multivalued diffeomorphism. In a certain sense, this Misner string serves as
the classical double copy of the Dirac string.
Note that it could be natural to ask if this exploration on the Misner string
gives rise to a one-form symmetry in (genuine) teleparallel theories, as per the
symmetry triangle in Fig. 3.1. Namely, a one-form symmetry that arises by
multivalued diffeomorphisms.14 We had briefly remarked about this possibility
in Chap. III, and if it is indeed viable, it will describe an interesting interaction
between the two ideas, generalized symmetries and CK duality. In this context,
a question one can ask is “Do symmetries double copy?”
4.7 Poisson Gauge Theory
We end with a brief remark on Poisson gauge theories, which arises by replacing
the gauge algebra of an ordinary gauge theory with a Poisson algebra. This is
implemented in, e.g., Ref. [158].
In a Poisson gauge theory, the gauge potentialAα carries just one index. The
field strength is given byF αβ =∂ αAβ−∂βAα +{Aα,Aβ}, where the nonlinear
term is the Poisson bracket betweenA α andA β. Infinitesimal Poisson gauge
transformations act on the Poisson gauge potential asA α7→A α−Dαξ, where
Dαξ=∂ αξ+{A α,ξ}. On the Poisson field strength, we haveF αβ7→F αβ +
{ξ,F αβ}. It is left as an exercise to define Poisson gauge theory via CKR and
explicitly establish proper gauge transformation behaviors as in Sec. 4.4, while
also constructing Poisson Wilson line 15 and its Magnus formula.
14Investigations into this direction are beyond the scope of the present work, but several
speculative remarks could be made. First, recall that the explicit breaking of the one-
form symmetry due to multivalued local Lorentz symmetry has mandated the existence of
fermions as spinning particles (Chap. III). Similarly, the explicit breaking of the one-form
symmetry due to multivalued diffeomorphisms, if exists, may mandate the existence of mas-
sive particles. In this case we encounter a reincarnation of the Poincar´ e charges (mass, spin)
in GR via the higher-form (topological surface operator) framework. Second, the Misner
string is famously endable on NUT charges. Hence, if a magnetic picture exists for this
hypothetical one-form symmetry, then the explicit breaking of it might mandate the exis-
tence of a NUT-charged object in our universe. Indeed, we will see a concrete configuration
representing a finite Misner string ending on opposite NUT charges in Chap. VII. Third,
it will again be interesting to note similarities with lattice systems (cf. Ref. [571]). Lastly,
it will also be natural to consider multivalued volume-preserving diffeomorphisms for SD
gravity in the frameworks of Secs. 2.4.3 or 2.5.3. Note that this seems to be what is envi-
sioned or suggested in Ref. [198], although the symmetry transformations were not explicitly
discussed.
15Note that, in noncommutative gauge theories, a gauge-invariant Wilsonloopis viable;

===== PAGE 194 =====
172
Although similar in spirit of CK duality, the status of Poisson gauge theory
seems more established than diffeomorphism gauge theory; see, e.g., Ref. [572–
576]. Part of this status may owe to the fact that Poisson gauge theory can
be obtained as the semi-commutative limit of noncommutative gauge theory,
which is a well-studied subject [548]. Note also that diffeomorphism gauge the-
ories onMare automatically Poisson gauge theories onT ∗Mby implementing
the diffeomorphism generator as the cotangent “momentum” [391, 575].
The semi-commutative limit of the Seiberg-Witten map [548] provides a
field redefinition from an ordinary abelian gauge connectiona α to a Poisson
gauge connectionA α, which is based on the Moser lemma [572, 573, 577].
It will be interesting to investigate if the semi-commutative limit of Seiberg-
Witten map (or its adequate volume-preserving diffeomorphism generalization,
if exists) can be useful in explicitly constructing the field redefinition from the
BI photon field to the manifest CK dual field basis, explaining the equivalence
BIBI∼= YMYM(sdiff(M)) in Eq. (4.10) in two (ord>2) dimensions. One lesson that
can be gained from this investigation will be the fate of gauge symmetries in the
problem of double copy. A key philosophy and feature of the Seiberg-Witten
map is that gauge orbits match between the two sides. One of the perennial
questions of double copy has been that whether one has to pursue an explicitly
gauge-fixed description to manifest double copy or aim to formulate everything
in terms of gauge-covariant or gauge-invariant objects (cf. the desire for field
basis independence during the NLSM discourse in Sec. 4.2.2).
4.A From Equations of Motion to Amplitudes
To see why formulating a theory as a BAS theory at the EoM level manifests
CK duality or double copy, consider the derivation of the scattering amplitudes
of BAS theory via BG recursion [154] (perturbiner method [155–157]), which
performs LSZ reduction on a one-point function evaluated on a background
[90]. This represents BAS amplitudes as a sum over cubic rooted tree graphs
Γ with momentum labels:

see, e.g., Refs. [551, 552].

same relation holds for the tilded color factors as ˜C(Γi) + ˜C(Γj) + ˜C(Γk) = 0
and vice versa. As a result, the BCJ definition of CK duality [87–89] is sat-
isfied if a choice for the Lie algebra ˜gexists such that ˜C(Γ) can define a tree
graph function of the external momenta (and also polarizations for spinning

j-particles; the inverse mappingπ a = (q·∂)−1jaµqµ is relevant for the root-leg
conversion of aj-particle to aπ-particle. Details are to be found in Ref. [90].

C h a p t e r 5
SIMPLICITY OF SELF-DUAL BLACK HOLE
In his classic monograph [262], Chandrasekhar described black holes as “the
most perfect macroscopic objects” in the universe. With the first direct detec-
tions of gravitational waves [9, 10] and the iconic horizon-scale image produced
by the Event Horizon Telescope [14], these theoretical objects have now turned
into precision experimental probes of strong-field gravity. It is in this sense
that the black hole may be viewed as “the atom of the twenty-first century”
[578]: simple enough to be universal, yet rich enough to reveal profound struc-
tures.
In the meantime, recent theoretical studies [163, 164, 175, 260, 261, 263–270]
have provided deeper investigations intoSD black holes, realizing their excep-
tional simplicity. This line of study may be traced back to the classic works
[272, 579–582] of Gibbons, Hawking, and Ruback on gravitational instantons
and hidden symmetries. The SD Taub-NUT solution, which is an instance of
a Gibbons-Hawking gravitational instanton [579, 580], has been investigated
as a prime example of a SD black hole (see, e.g., Refs. [175, 261, 264] for jus-
tifications of this terminology). In light of its associated hidden symmetry,
it has been suggested that the SD Taub-NUT solution is “the analog of the
hydrogen atom for black holes” [266]. Moreover, the classical integrability of
SD gravity [313] renders its perturbation theory tractable [264]; for instance,
it has been shown that the SD Taub-NUT solution exhibits a graviton helicity
selection rule up to multiplicity two [261, 264].
Certainly, SD black holes could be viewed as purely mathematical: the self-
duality condition inevitably makes their gravitational fields complex-valued.
See the discussions on complex spacetimes in Refs. [312–315, 328–330], for
instance. One may at least regard them as formal idealizations leading to
theoretical curiosities, while keeping in mind that the ultimate desire will be
to restore the missing ASD part either perturbatively or nonperturbatively
(recall Secs. 2.4 and 2.5).
To give the reader at least a concrete glimpse of why the SD black hole
has been compared to the hydrogen atom [266] and why they are of an active
theoretical interest [163, 164, 175, 260, 261, 263–270], this chapter aims to

===== PAGE 198 =====
176
analyze a simpler yet analogous system in electromagnetism: the SD dyon. As
is established by Ref. [163] and will be reproduced in Chap. VI of this thesis,
the SD dyon has been shown to describe the exact gauge theory counterpart
of the SD Taub-NUT solution via the Kerr-Schild double copy. See also works
[261, 263, 264] and discussions therein. The SD dyon has served as a simplified
testing ground for questions related to SD black holes [263, 583].
Our conclusion in this chapter will be that the motion of a charged test
particle in the SD dyon background ismaximally superintegrable. Note that
later parts of this thesis will extend this superintegrability to SD black hole
backgrounds and to the all-orders-in-spin dynamics of spinning probe particles.

5.2 Self-Dual Dyon as the Hydrogen Atom
A SD dyon is a dyon whose electric and magnetic charges satisfy the relation
Q=iQ ⋆. It sources a SD electromagnetic field: ⃗E=i ⃗B.
An ASD dyon is a dyon whose electric and magnetic charges satisfy the
relationQ=−iQ ⋆. It sources an ASD electromagnetic field: ⃗E=−i ⃗B.
Together, they are referred to as chiral dyons. Clearly, these chiral limits
are the extremal cases in which the parameterγin Eq. (5.10) becomes zero.
From now on, we study the motion of the test particle in the background
of a chiral dyon. As mentioned earlier, this may be seen as a rather bizarre
pathway from a conservative point of view. Due to the imaginary magnetic
charge, the particle experiences a complex-valued Lorentz force. Hence its
velocity becomes complex, and even its position becomes complex; otherwise
there is no solution to the classical EoM. However, this complexified limit turns
out to be intriguing enough to pursue, at least as a formal curiosity.
First of all, upon imposing the chiral limitγ→0, Eq. (5.9) boils down to
γ= 0 =⇒ m

superintegrable. A further computation shows that the dynamical symmetry
algebra is nicely closed, describingso(4) forE 2 <m 2,so(1,3) forE 2 >m 2 (or
so(4,C) in the complexified category), andiso(3) forE 2 =m 2.
In these concrete senses, we conclude that the dynamics of a relativistic
test particle in the background of a chiral dyon is isomorphic to that of the
nonrelativistic Hydrogen atom or Kepler problem.

in the Lorentz-covariant notation. The underlying geometric structures are
identified as the Killing-Yano tensorY µν(x) and the ASD Pleba` nski two-forms
(Σa)µν. It is left as an exercise to understand how the quaternionic structure
in Eq. (5.26) boils down to the symmetry algebra.
Notably, the same geometric structures arise for the SD Taub-NUT black
hole as well. This forms the basis of the statement [266] that the SD Taub-
NUT black hole is secretly the hydrogen atom.
More details can be found in the next chapter, where it is shown that the
SD Taub-NUT black hole is the double copy of the SD dyon. Specifically, we
discover a new metric for the SD Taub-NUT solution by double copying the
Kerr-Schild gauge potential of the SD dyon.

===== PAGE 204 =====
182
C h a p t e r 6
SELF-DUAL BLACK HOLE FROM DOUBLE COPY
We derive a previously unknown KS metric for the SD Taub-NUT solution by
classical double copy. We show its equivalence with the well-known Gibbons-
Hawking instanton metric by means of an explicit complex coordinate trans-
formation. Our KS metric develops a semi-infinite string defect as the Misner
string, much like the original metric. It also establishes two more classical dou-
ble copy correspondences to the SD dyon in electromagnetism, one of which is
a novel nonlocal kind based on propagators between dyonic matter.
Contents of Secs. 6.1 to 6.6 are adapted from Ref. [163], J.-H. Kim, “Single Kerr-
Schild metric for Taub-Newman-Unti-Tamburino instanton,”Phys. Rev. D111
no. 2, (2025) L021703,arXiv:2405.09518 [hep-th].
6.1 Introduction
Recently, an exciting program dubbed classical double copy [161–174, 181–
185] has refined analogies between gravity and electromagnetism into concrete
mappings between classical solutions while being rooted in the study of scat-
tering amplitudes [86–89, 327]. The prime example is the KS double copy,
established in the seminal work [160]. 1 It is shown that a stationary KS solu-
tiong=η+ Φℓ⊗ℓin GR defines a stationary solution in Maxwell theory by
the gauge potentialA= Φℓ, which takes a “single copy” of the null one-form
ℓ. Conversely, one envisions the gravitational counterpartg=η+ Φℓ⊗ℓof
a stationary solutionA= Φℓin electromagnetism, by taking a “double copy”
ofℓ. Well-known instances of this correspondence are Schwarzschild to point
charge and Kerr to a rotating solution dubbed “
p
Kerr ” [98, 160, 180, 202].
From this angle, it is natural to expect that the Taub-NUT solution [596,
597] could correspond to a dyon, given the interpretation of the NUT charge
as the gravitomagnetic monopole [172, 294, 474–476, 486, 489, 491]. Unfortu-
nately, only adoubleKS metricg=η+ Φ 1ℓ1⊗ℓ 1 + Φ2ℓ2⊗ℓ 2 has been known
to exist for the Taub-NUT solution: a classic result due to Pleba´ nski and
1KS metrics give an abelian sector of GR in the sense of the effective linearization
property [177, 178]. In this light, the KS double copy may be viewed as probing the double
copy correspondence between the abelian sectors of YM theory and GR.

===== PAGE 205 =====
183
Demia´ nski [331, 332, 598]. Consequently, the anticipated correspondence be-
tween the Taub-NUT solution and the electromagnetic dyon had to be achieved
only by an alternative construction using the double KS ansatz [181].
Remarkably, the present letter points out that a direct application of the
original KS double copy is viable for theSDTaub-NUT solution. This is the
extremal case of the Taub-NUT solution that has recently gained a revived
interest [260, 261, 263, 265, 266].
Crucially, we discover that the SD Taub-NUT solution admits asingleKS
metric:g=η+ Φℓ⊗ℓ. First, a KS metric is obtained by KS double copying
the gauge potential of the SD dyon in electromagnetism. We then show that it
describes the SD Taub-NUT solution by explicitly constructing a coordinate
transformation to the well-known Gibbons-Hawking [579, 580] instanton met-
ric. To our understanding, this gives the first instance of the classical double
copy program yielding a new result in GR (a previously unknown metric).
Next, the physical interpretation of the metric as a gravitational dyon is
established within the KS description. Most importantly, we confirm the char-
acteristicMisner stringgeometry: the gravitational analog [474, 475] of the
Dirac string [454, 455]. Notably, finding its distributional source [475, 476]
becomes a significantly simpler problem in KS coordinates, thanks to the ef-
fective linearization property [176–178].
Lastly, the classical double copy is established in two more ways. Our KS
description concretely validates the Weyl double copy proposed by Refs. [182–
184], which has been stated without referencing an explicit metric. We then
propose a new exotic form of classical double copy employing anonlocalop-
erator, which arises from propagators between dyonic matter [456, 599–602].
In this chapter, we use the field theorists’ natural unit in which 8πG= 1.

In electromagnetism, a dyon is a point particle carrying both electric and
magnetic charges (recall Eq. (5.1)). The SD dyon is a dyon with electric and
magnetic charges, say,Qand−iQ. Its gauge potential can be given as

An important remark is in order. The astute reader may have noticed an
unusual feature of the diffeomorphism in Eq. (6.5): it describes acomplex-
ified diffeomorphism, 2 sinceζis frozen while ˜ζgets scrambled: hence the
notation ˜ζinstead of ¯ζ. This is justified as a legitimate operation in the
current context because the Lorentzian-signature SD Taub-NUT solution is
2Such a complexification is exactly what is practiced in Pleba´ nski [331], Sec. 7, and Ple-

which Penrose makes in Ref. [312]. See also Refs. [313–315, 328–330] for related discussions
and reviews.

Eq. (6.3) solves complexified Einstein’s equations governing holomorphic met-
rics [258, 261, 314, 315, 328]. As a consequence of this interpretation,ζand ˜ζ
can be transformed independently asholomorphiccoordinates.

6.4 Physical Interpretation
We should now examine the physical validity of the KS metric in Eq. (6.3). To
begin with, we point out that Eq. (6.3)itselfis quite unusual as a KS metric.
While the KS potential is simply Φ =M/4πr, the null one-formℓinvolves a
semi-infinite string defectalong the negativez-axis, which is exactly how the
gravitomagnetic charge is encoded in this metric:
ℓ=−dt+ x+iy
r+z dx+ y−ix
r+z dy+dz.(6.13)
This is intriguing, as the null one-form is usually regular wherever the KS
potential is regular—which is especially true for the double KS construction
of Taub-NUT [181].
We expect that the interpretation of this string defect should be theMisner
string[474, 475], just as in the original metric in Eq. (6.6): the gravitational
analog of the Dirac string, inputting a gravitomagnetic flux of−imat the
origin. To show this, we recall that there are at least three senses in which the
Taub-NUT metric [474, 475] describes a gravitational analog of a dyon: (a) the
Misner string is invisible as a coordinate artifact if the gravitational analog of
the Dirac quantization condition holds [474], (b) the Misner string implements
a flux tube of time monodromy [172, 294, 486], and (c) the distributional stress-
energy of the string describes an energy-less thin solenoid of mass current
[475, 476]. Our goal now is to show that all of these properties hold in the KS
description as well.
Firstly, we derive the Dirac quantization condition. For the SD dyon gauge
potential in Eq. (6.2), adding a term (Q/2π)dlogζrepositions its Dirac string
along the positivez-axis (north pole). This encodes the complexified group-
valued transition functionζ ieQ/2π, whereeis the electromagnetic coupling.
Demanding its single-valuedness on the “equator” [25] impliesieQ∈2πZ.
The gravitational Dirac quantization can be derived in a similar fashion, i.e.,
by hiding the strings with overlapping coordinate patches [172, 294, 474, 486].
In the KS description, we find that the diffeomorphism
t7→t+ M
2π logζ, ˜ζ7→ 1−(M/4πr)(1 + ˜ζζ)/ ˜ζζ
1 + (M/4πr)(1 + ˜ζζ)
˜ζ (6.14)
withrandζfixed transforms the KS metric in Eq. (6.4) to another KS metric:
ds2 =−dt 2 +dr 2 +r 2 4d ˜ζdζ
(1 + ˜ζζ) 2 + M

which develops a string defect along the positivez-axis (north pole). For this
diffeomorphism to be single-valued within the overlapping region, the time
coordinate should be periodic ast∼t+iM, given the 2πiperiodicity of
logζ. Hence the energyeof a test particle is quantized asieM∈2πZ. This
is precisely the gravitational Dirac quantization condition due to the NUT
charge−iM.
Secondly, we study the near-string geometries in an explicit manner. Let
ξ:=x+iy, ˜ξ:=x−iybe complex coordinates for the (complexified)x-yplane,
and suppose a positive infinitesimalϵ. Applying the replacementz7→−1/ϵ+z
to the gauge potential in Eq. (6.2) and then taking the limitϵ→0, we obtain
A= (Q/2π)dlog ˜ξ. This precisely describes an infinite Dirac string generated
by a large gauge transformation [31].
For the line element in Eq. (6.3), the same limiting procedure leaves out a

t′ =t+ M
2π log ˜ξ.(6.16)
Namely, in the vicinity of the string, the geometry approaches the image of
empty spacetime under a large diffeomorphism. Here, a Sagnac interferometer
measures a monodromy in time as the gravitational AB phase [483, 486, 489,
490], localized along thez-axis (as ˜ξ= 0). This precisely describes an infinite
Misner string as a thin tube of gravitomagnetic flux−iM. 3
3One might make a parallel between the field strengthF=dAand the torsion two-form
ddt′ (cf. Refs. [460, 519, 520]) associated with these two strings, which are distributional.

===== PAGE 211 =====
189
Thirdly, we derive the distributional stress-energy tensor of this string.
Among various approaches [603–605], we particularly consider the “harmonic-
gauge linearization” of Ref. [176] which stationary KS metrics enjoy as a lux-
ury. Provided the congruence ofℓ µ∂µ is geodesic and shear-free (GSF), this
approach identifiesT µν =u (µJν) as the source for the double copy solution
[176]. HereJ µ is the single copy source, whileu µ =δµ0 is the unit timelike
Killing vector. For our case, the GSF property of the congruence associated
with the null one-form in Eq. (6.13) can be established from an identity
∂µℓν = 1
2R

ηµν +ℓ (µ(2u−ℓ) ν)−iε µνρσuρℓσ

,(6.17)
while the single copy sourceJ µ easily follows by acting on the Laplacian□:=
−ηµν∂µ∂ν to Eq. (6.1). Applying the formulaT µν =u (µJν) then immediately
reproduces Bonnor’s stress-energy tensor [475, 476], describing a point mass
mattached to a massless semi-infinite rotating rod.
To sum up, we have convinced ourselves from multiple angles that the KS
metric in Eq. (6.3) indeed exhibits the geometrical and physical characteris-
tics of a Taub-NUT spacetime arising from the Misner string. This analysis
demonstrates that the KS description of the solution is not merely a formal
construct but physical. Moreover, the quick derivation of the distributional
source demonstrates its usefulness as well.
6.5 More Double Copy Correspondences
Finally, for interested readers, we discuss alternative classical double copy
relations that can be drawn between SD Taub-NUT and SD dyon. The possible
options are Weyl double copy [182–184], operator KS double copy [160, 161,
169], and SD double copy [160, 327].
To this end, we first note that our KS metric for the SD Taub-NUT solution
describes an elegant formula in the spinor notation: Eq. (6.3) translates to

6.5.1 Weyl Double Copy
TheWeyl double copy[182–184] seeks to directly construct the Weyl curvature
of four-dimensional algebraically special spacetimes by “squaring” electromag-
netic field strengths via a choice of a scalar field (“zeroth copy field”) while
not referencing a metric nor a gauge potential. It has been formulated in both
position [182] and dual twistor [183, 184] spaces.
Below, we establish that the SD Taub-NUT solution is the Weyl double copy
of the SD dyon in both position space and dual twistor space senses.
First of all, direct computation based on Eqs. (6.2) and (6.3) shows that the
SD curvature tensors (field strength and Weyl) of SD dyon and SD Taub-NUT
solutions are given by

Previously, the above squaring relations for the SD Taub-NUT solution
had been speculated on in Refs. [183, 184]. The argument was that the SD
Taub-NUT solution would describe the “SD part” of Schwarzschild, so its SD
Weyl curvature and principal spinors might be exactly equated to those of the
Schwarzschild solution. Although physically plausible, it should be pointed
out that this claim is strictly speaking a statement whose validity is restricted
within the linearized level. Otherwise, it is a highly nontrivial assertion in
light of the nonlinearity of GR demanding an extra effort for justification.
The clarification which our explicit KS metric in Eq. (6.3) brings is that
Eq. (6.20) has in fact described theexactWeyl tensor of the SD Taub-NUT
solution at the fully nonlinear level. The Cartesian coordinatesx ˙ααof the flat
background employed in the Penrose transform in Eq. (6.22) can be identified
with the KS coordinates for the exact curved SD Taub-NUT geometry. 6 Con-
sequently, the Weyl double copy construction of the SD Taub-NUT solution is
validated at the fully nonlinear orders.
Generally speaking, it seems that the validity of the Weyl double copy could
only be strictly ensured at the nonlinear orders when it can be assisted by an
underlying KS structure at the metric level, in which case one can appeal to
the effective linearization property [177, 178].

6.5.3 Self-Dual Double Copy
Computation shows that our KS coordinates are also the Pleba´ nski coordi-
nates [314, 320] for the second heavenly equation, on which the SD double
copy [160, 161, 327] is based. The SD double copy could draw fascinating
conclusions about the SD dyon and Taub-NUT solutions in the context of
Moyal deformation of the heavenly equation [158, 159, 323–326]. Strangely,
however, the SD Taub-NUT metric is not straightforwardly the SD double
copy of SD dyon in terms of the Pleba´ nski scalar field:A α˙α =o α∂0 ˙α(qζ/4π),

===== PAGE 216 =====
194
hα˙αβ˙β =o αoβ∂0 ˙α∂0 ˙β(m(2r+z)ζ 2/24π). This puzzle deserves further investi-
gation, in comparison with the Eguchi-Hanson case [161].
6.6 Conclusions
In this work, we derived a KS metric for the SD Taub-NUT solution by classi-
cal double copy and validated it by an explicit complexified coordinate trans-
formation. The KS metric exhibits the Misner string defect, supporting its
physical interpretation. Overall, we established three concrete senses in which
the SD Taub-NUT solution is the gravitational counterpart of the SD dyon
in electromagnetism: KS, Weyl, and nonlocal operator KS classical double
copies. The last one adds to the inventory of classical double copies as a new
entry, whose physical origin is propagators between dyonic matter.
The distinctive charm of this work lies in the exciting development along
the very first three equations. The usual punchline of classical double copy has
been starting with a gravitational solution and then deducing the electromag-
netic counterpart. This work, in contrast, takes a reverse path: we constructed
an electromagnetic solution and then applied the double copy to yield a new
statement in GR.
It is also insightful to frame this work in the angle of “classical double copy
of gravitational instantons.” The only known instance has been Ref. [161]’s
work on the Eguchi-Hanson instanton solution [616–618], which traces back
to Refs. [365, 619]. This work adds another instance to the discourse, namely
the Taub-NUT instanton. It is amusing that the correspondence between SD
objects in electromagnetism and gravity which the Gibbons-Hawking ansatz
[579, 580] had suggested a long time ago becomes strengthened from new
perspectives by the advent of the classical double copy program.
The KS metric of the SD Taub-NUT solution conveys an aesthetic ele-
gance in terms of its minimalist and spinorial nature. We anticipate that this
new metric will contribute to future studies on the SD Taub-NUT solution,
both conceptually and practically. For further consequences or applications,
we suggest the following directions: asymptotic symmetries [620, 621], in-
tegrability of test-particle motion [272, 582], black hole perturbation theory
[240, 261, 266, 622], exact graviton propagators [623–625], or self-force calcula-
tions [626–629] in an idealized setup. Especially, explorations in the scattering
context are likely to shed insights on the Kerr black hole scattering for an in-

===== PAGE 217 =====
195
tegrable subsector, on account of the Newman-Janis shift [202]: the “SD part”
of Kerr is a SD Taub-NUT solution [174, 256, 257, 260, 261, 630].

6.B A Puzzle Regarding Two Classical Double Copies
The amplitudes-level double copy of BCJ [87] implies that the perturbiner
(Duff [631]) constructions of classical solutions in YM theory and gravity can
exhibit a correspondence through replacing the color Lie algebra with a kine-
matic Lie algebra, whose identity is known in the SD sector [327] but not
beyond. In the meantime, the classical double copy originally proposed by
Monteiro and O’Connell [160] has resorted to the effective linearization prop-
erty [177, 178] of KS metrics.
To our best understanding, an explicit demonstration of the former implying
the latter has been not given so far. First, the KS double copy program has
not discussed a faithful diagram-by-diagram mapping at the nonlinear orders.
Second, such a mapping is unlikely to be identified easily after all, due to
the mysterious status of the kinematic algebra beyond the SD sector. Hence
to address the question of whether the amplitudes double copy implies the
classical double copy, it could be more promising to first focus on the SD
sector (cf. Ref. [632]).
In this context, Ref. [163] identified a puzzle that there seems to be a dis-
agreement between the amplitudes double copy and the classical double copy.
On the one hand, Ref. [163]’s KS metric shows that the SD Taub-NUT so-
lution is the classical double copy [160] of the SD dyon, which describes a
correspondence between type [1,1] and type D solutions. On the other hand,
the amplitudes double copy via the second heavenly equation [160] works by
taking the same Pleba´ nski scalar for both gauge theory and gravity up to at-
taching a constant color factor. Ref. [163] explicitly constructs the Pleba´ nski
scalars for the SD dyon and the SD Taub-NUT solutions and finds mismatch.
Taking lessons from a recent work [175], we realize that the mapping stip-

===== PAGE 219 =====
197
ulated by the amplitudes double copy generically describes a correspondence
between type [2] (null Maxwell field) and type D solutions. This is because
Tod [365]’s construction essentially concerns the linearized case of the second
heavenly equation. This validates the mismatch pointed out in this chapter
[163]. Consequently, we learn that all SD black holes seem to have “two square
roots.”

===== PAGE 220 =====
198
C h a p t e r 7
NEWMAN-JANIS ALGORITHM FROM
TAUB-NUT INSTANTONS
It is shown that the Kerr metric represents the nonlinear superposition of self-
dual and anti-self-dual Taub-NUT instantons. This promotes the Newman-
Janis algorithm to a rigorous derivation of the Kerr metric with a definite
physical origin. In the same way, the Kerr-Newman and charged Kerr-Taub-
NUT solutions are systems of Taub-NUT instantons and chiral dyons.
This chapter reproduces the contents of Ref. [164], J.-H. Kim, “Newman-Janis algo-
rithm from Taub-Newman-Unti-Tamburino instantons,”Phys. Rev. Lett.136no. 23,
(2026) 231401,arXiv:2412.19611 [gr-qc].
7.1 Introduction
The NJA [202] is a mystery in the history of relativity. Discovered about sixty
years ago, the NJA aims to derive the rotating black hole solution of Kerr [633]
by applying a “complex coordinate transformation” to the static black hole
solution of Schwarzschild. Apparently, this procedure is a sequence of ad hoc
manipulations. Each element in the Schwarzschild metric must be promoted
to specific combinations of complex variables, which are then transformed by a
rule of unknown physical origin. Hence the NJA has stood merely as a formal
mathematical trick.
Such a lack of clear understanding is unsettling because the NJA holds the
historical significance as the very method that led to the discovery of the
Kerr-Newman metric [179]: the charged rotating black hole solution.
Despite early skepticism—epitomized by characterizations such as a “fluke”
[243] or “methods which transcend logic” [634]—research efforts have persisted
over decades toward a satisfactory justification [98, 163, 174, 207–209, 226,
242–260, 329, 330, 479, 634–673]. These works may be classified into three
categories, as outlined below.
The first category could be namedclassical explanations. Here, the goal
is to justify the NJA from geometrical perspectives. This means to resolve
inherent ambiguities, identify hidden assumptions, and explore simplifications

===== PAGE 221 =====
199
or generalizations. Notable contributions in this direction include works by
Talbot [242], Drake and Szekeres [243], G¨ urses and G¨ ursey [246], Flaherty [247,
330], Rajan and Visser [248], and Giampieri [249]. The original article [202]
also provides a brief justification along this direction: an allowed possibility
in Kerr theorem [611]. These approaches could be agnostic about physical
origins, however.
The second category describesthe works of Newman himself[252–259, 329,
330], which portray a more serious stance. Emphasis is put on the fact that
the NJA realizes the spin of Kerr black hole via an imaginary displacement
into complexified spacetime. The NJA is treated not as a mere mathematical
curiosity but a signal of a deeper physical structure, such as a Hodge duality
on angular momenta as mass dipole moments [252]. A fundamental unification
of spin and spacetime is envisioned from the construction of “complex center
of mass” [252–259, 330, 672].
Lastly, the third category describes themodern explanationemerged through
works [98, 206–209]. Here, one adopts a particle physicist’s perspective and
imagines observing the Kerr black hole by throwing gravitons (i.e., through
gravitational waves) from far away, as if one detects the internal structure
of a proton by firing electrons in a particle collider. In terms of scattering
cross-sections, it is revealed that the Kerr black hole behaves like an ide-
ally pointlike object when the incident gravitons are all circularly polarized
in either right or left handedness, i.e., self-dual (SD) or anti-self-dual (ASD)
[98, 207–212, 221, 222]. Thus, despite the black hole’s extended geometrical
features such as the ring singularity or the horizon, its “graviton X-ray image”
displays just two objects: a point that absorbs SD gravitons only and another
point that absorbs ASD gravitons only. This stunning simplicity is identified
as the basis of NJA [98], as the coordinates of such points precisely predict
Newman’s complex centers of mass by taking complex values.
The modern explanation shows that the NJA is not a mere mathematical
construct but rather a representation of some physical structure: the NJA is
recast as a statement about scattering amplitudes, a gauge-invariant physical
observable. That said, it did not decipher every aspect of the NJA in its
original, relativist form as a statement about bulk metrics. The conversion of
the scattering amplitudes to bulk metrics is based on perturbative methods
[142, 176–178, 631], so a resummed picture is lacking.

===== PAGE 222 =====
200
=
Figure 7.1: In electromagnetism, a current loop behaves like a pair of opposite
monopoles: Amp` erian and Gilbertian dipoles [613]. This letter reveals a gravita-
tional version of this duality.
A similar caveat applies to the fact attributed to Crawley, Guevara, Miller,
and Strominger [260] or Ghezelbash, Mann, and Sorkin [630], which is limited
to the SD sector and thus fall short of fully explaining the nonlinear aspect of
NJA with both SD and ASD components.
In this letter, we provide a principled derivation of the Kerr metric that
faithfully reproduces the NJA in its original form. This not only identifies
a definite physical origin for structures observed in the classical explanations
and Newman’s works but also reveals a fully nonlinear, nonperturbative picture
completing the modern explanation.
The key new finding is that the Kerr metric describes a pair of SD and
ASD Taub-Newman-Unti-Tamburino (NUT) instantons, which are known to
be gravitational analogs of chiral dyons. These instantons are exactly the ide-
ally pointlike objects in the field theorists’ graviton X-ray image. The Kerr
metric is derived by establishing a mathematical theorem about nonlinear su-
perposition of Kerr-Schild (KS) spacetimes. This builds upon a crucial recent
finding [163] and a modern framework known as KS double copy [160]. Our
construction generalizes to charged Kerr-Taub-NUT solutions, thus validating
the historical derivation [179] of the Kerr-Newman metric as well.
7.2 Intuition and Overview
The prototype of this chapter grew out of a moment of pure imagination
that struck the present author some years ago. In electromagnetism, a mag-
netic dipole arises from not only an Amp` erian current loop but also a static
monopole pair, like the N and S poles of a bar magnet (Fig. 7.1). By anal-
ogy, the question was whether the ring singularity of Kerr black hole can be
dualized into a pair of gravitomagnetic monopoles.

===== PAGE 223 =====
201
Figure 7.2: The established fact due to Gibbons and Hawking [579, 580] concerns
the nonlinear superposition of Taub-NUT instantons of the same chirality. The new
fact proved in this work concerns the nonlinear superposition of two Taub-NUT
instantons of the opposite chirality.
It is known [331, 474–476, 479, 485, 674] that the gravitational analog of
the monopole is the Taub-NUT [596, 597] solution. This is a stationary vac-
uum solution characterized by two parameters: massMand magnetic mass
N, which are gravitoelectric and gravitomagnetic charges, respectively. The
metric of the Taub-NUT solution develops a semi-infinite line defect as the
gravitational Dirac string, dubbed Misner string [474, 475].
A modern framework known as KS double copy [160] has elevated such
analogies to exact mathematical correspondences between stationary vacuum
solutions in Maxwell theory and GR. For example, the Coulomb solution is the
counterpart of the Schwarzschild solution. It has been recently shown [163]
that this correspondence applies to the Taub-NUT solution as well, yet only
in chiral (SD or ASD) limits in whichM=±iN.
The chiral limits of the Taub-NUT solution are referred to as Taub-NUT
instantons. Historically, they were introduced by the seminal works of Gibbons
and Hawking [579, 580] as paradigmatic instances of gravitational instantons;
hence the name.

===== PAGE 224 =====
202
Wick−−−−→
Figure 7.3: The ring singularity of Kerr black hole factorizes into a pair of SD and
ASD Taub-NUT instantons.
A previouslyknown factis that any number of Taub-NUT instantons of
the same chirality can be put together to yield a multi-centered exact solution
[579, 580]. Thenew factwe prove is that SD and ASD Taub-NUT instantons
can form a two-centered exact solution: opposite chiralities. Remarkably, this
two-centered solution is nothing other than the Kerr solution.
7.3 Factorization of Kerr
As an introduction, we begin with an innocuous mathematical fact about a
quartic polynomial. Consider the zero locus
(x2 +y 2 +z 2−a 2)2 + (2az)2 = 0,(7.1)
whereais a constant. Eq. (7.1) describes aringof radiusain the three-
dimensional space (x,y,z)∈R 3. Intriguingly, this ring can also appear as a
set oftwo disjoint points. Suppose we allowx,y,zin Eq. (7.1) to take complex
values so that we obtain a complex algebraic variety in the affine spaceC 3.
Then the ring is the slice of this variety by the real sectionx,y,z∈R. However,
consider the slicex,y,z∈iR. Wick-rotatingx,y,zin Eq. (7.1) gives
(x2 +y 2 +z 2 +a 2)2−(2az) 2 = 0,(7.2)
which factorizes to (x 2 +y 2 + (z+a) 2)(x2 +y 2 + (z−a) 2) = 0. This admits
just two points as solutions: (0,0,±a).
The astute reader will point out that Eq. (7.1) is the very equation of the
ring singularity of the Kerr black hole. The Kerr metric is characterized by
massMand ring radiusa. In KS coordinates, for instance, it reads

diffeomorphism. Such a line defect of time monodromy is the very notion of
Misner string as a gravitomagnetic flux tube [163, 172, 486, 521, 566, 674].

Note that the magnetic mass dipole moment computes to⃗J= (−iM/2)(2i⃗ a) =

fact, the static configuration in Eq. (7.8) reproduces all the black hole’s spin-
induced mass multipole moments for finite⃗ a, which are known to take the
same magnitude with alternating signs [200–204, 256] 2.
The separation 2i⃗ ahas to be pure-imaginary for ⃗Jto be real, since Taub-

fact, we will learn that the imaginary coordinates±i⃗ aare exactly the imagi-
nary displacements of the NJA. Therefore, we realize that the NJA is a state-
ment about the curvature singularity of Kerr solution when taken as a complex
saddle.
It should be stressed that our analysis above does not count on any methods
of linearization or perturbative expansions. It is readily performed in other
coordinate systems as well, such as the Boyer-Lindquist system.
To our knowledge, the above explicit proof of the exact equivalence between
Kerr and a dipolar configuration of Taub-NUT instantons is new, although an
argument appears in Ref. [675]. Our result is also disparate from the “black
dihole” of Refs. [676–688].

Next, we regard the Taub-NUT instantons aspartsand show that they can
be assembled together to form the Kerr black hole:

A crucial feature of Einstein’s gravity theory, however, is nonlinearity of field
equations. Hence generally speaking, it is virtually impossible to assemble
two vacuum solutions together to obtain another by a simple process. The +
symbol in Eq. (7.9) signifies anonlinearsum.
Yet remarkably, the Taub-NUT instanton solution admits a KS metric [163]
as mentioned earlier. For instance, the KS metric of a SD Taub-NUT instanton
is given in an elegant formula in the spinor notation:

gauge and physical data are respectively stored in the ASD and SD spinors.
The Weyl tensor is purely SD. See Ref. [163] for the derivation of Eq. (7.10)
from the well-known Gibbons-Hawking metric [579, 580].
Notably, KS metrics are known to enjoy aneffective linearizationproperty:
the exact vacuum Einstein’s equations are solved if the linearized equations
are solved [176–178]. Via this effective linearization, two vacuum KS metrics
can be assembled into another as shown below.
First of all, a NVFℓin complexified Minkowski space iscanonicalif it
is stationary, GSF, and unit-normalized asℓ 0 = 1 with respect to the time
direction. One can treat canonical NVFs as projective objects, due to the
fixed normalizationℓ 0 = 1.
Each canonical NVF is associated with two solutions. Thesingle copy so-
lutionis the electromagnetic gauge potentialA µ =θℓ µ. Thedouble copy
solutionis the metricg µν =η µν +ϵθℓ µℓν, whereϵis an auxiliary parameter.
Provided thatθ=∂ µℓµ is the expansion of the congruence ofℓ,A µ solves vac-
uum Maxwell’s equations in flat spacetime whileg µν solves vacuum Einstein’s
equations.
The above set of definitions is our formulation of the framework known as
KS double copy [160].

where we have written down the inverse metric for a direct comparison with
Ref. [202] while settingϵ=M.
Remarkably, the vacuum solution in Eq. (7.16) is exactly the Kerr solution
with massMand angular momentum ⃗J=−iM(⃗ x1−⃗ x2)/2, centered around
(⃗ x1 +⃗ x2)/2.
In particular, take⃗ x1 = +i⃗ aand⃗ x2 =−i⃗ afor⃗ a= (0,0,a). Then Eq. (7.16)
exactly coincides with the Kerr inverse metric obtained in the very article
[202].
This establishes a mathematically rigorous derivation of the Kerr solution
as the nonlinear superposition of SD and ASD Taub-NUT instantons.
7.6 Newman-Janis Algorithm Deciphered
It remains to explicate that the above derivation faithfully reproduces the
NJA. Consider the inverse metric of the Schwarzschild solution, identified as
the nonlinear superposition of SD and ASD Taub-NUT instantons overlapped
at the same point:

This operation precisely reproduces the NJA in its very original formulation

(7) of Ref. [202] areliterallyreproduced upon using spheroidal coordinates.
In Ref. [202], the±i⃗ aimaginary shifts of the spinors (null tetrad) and the
“complexification” of the KS potential 2/|⃗ x|→2Re|⃗ x−i⃗ a|−1 are posited as ad
hoc rules. The nonlinear superposition theorem shows that this set of replace-
ments, i.e., the NJA, is a strict implication of the fact that the Schwarzschild-
Kerr family of solutions are systems of SD and ASD Taub-NUT instantons.
This refined understanding implies the following clarifications on the NJA.
First, the NJA isnota coordinate transformation as originally conceived
[202]. The instanton pairs with zero and nonzero separations, i.e., Schwarzschild
and Kerr, are never diffeomorphic.
Second, the NJA is not about one object as originally conceived [256, 257],
buttwo. It does not merely shifts a Schwarzschild black hole but insteadsplits
it into SD and ASD parts and thenseparatesthem, as is illustrated in Fig. 7.4.
The displacements⃗ x1,⃗ x2 are alsoindependent, although a reality condition
can be imposed a posteriori.
Third, it is important to explicitly address the nonlinear gravitational inter-
action between the SD and ASD instantons. Otherwise the NJA is not fully
explained nor justified as a method that generates an exact solution in Ein-

===== PAGE 231 =====
209
Figure 7.5: Implementing pure mass (left) and magnetic mass (right) with Taub-
NUT instantons.
stein gravity. Newman’s complex center of mass analysis [252, 256, 257], for
instance, identifies one worldline from the SD sector (in linearized gravity) and
does not elaborate much on the validity of “plus complex conjugate” in the
nonlinear theory. Works [260, 630] have shown that the SD Kerr-Taub-NUT
solution describes a SD Taub-NUT instanton, which is not a statement about
the Kerr solution which the NJA concerns. To reiterate, the NJA is abouttwo
Taub-NUT instantons, not just one SD instanton.
The technical structure of the formula in Eq. (7.14) may have been effectively
observed in works [244, 246, 248], but the instanton pair interpretation is not
realized there.

7.8 Summary
This chapter elevates the NJA to a rigorous derivation of rotating black hole
solutions with a definite origin: factorizations into SD and ASD Taub-NUT
instantons and chiral dyons.
This resolution of an old mystery will spark new perspectives on modern
theoretical studies of rotating black holes: hidden symmetries [233, 266, 586,

7.A Nonlinear Superposition Theorem
The idea of patching SD and ASD spacetimes together has been a long-
standing aspiration [344–346].7 Here, we tackle this problem in a narrow setup:
stationary KS spacetimes.
The complexified Minkowski spaceMis the linear spaceC 4 equipped with a
holomorphic flat metricηwith a signature-(−,+,+,+) real section. Letx µ be
Cartesian coordinates. Letu µ be a unit vector such thatu 2 =η µνuµuν =−1;
this is the characterization of the stationary directionu µ =δ µ0 in the main
article. All indices are raised and lowered with the flat metricη.

such thatℓ 2 =η µνℓµℓν = 0.

shear-free, and unit-normalized as−u·ℓ= 1.

By repeating the above exercise, one systematically derives the NJA for

pair structure elucidated in the main article. In other words, the origin of the
NJA is the factorization of black holes into Taub-NUT instantons and chiral
dyons.
