C h a p t e r 8
GEODESIC DEVIATION TO ALL ORDERS
We establish an in-in formalism for geodesic deviation as an alternative to
Synge calculus, based on a covariant calculus of differential forms in tangent
bundle. This derives the exact Lagrangian and equations governing the fi-
nite geodesic deviation between a free-falling test particle and an arbitrary
observer, in terms of infinite sums whose coefficients are products of bino-
mial coefficients. Explicit expressions are provided up to tenth order, finding
agreements with the previous fourth-order result.
This section reproduces the contents of Ref. [695], J.-H. Kim, “Geodesic deviation
to all orders via a tangent bundle formalism,”General Relativity and Gravitation
58no. 7, (2026) 75,arXiv:2509.23600 [gr-qc].
8.1 Introduction
The GDE is a foundational topic in GR, commonly covered in textbooks [20,
291, 696–700]. First derived in 1927 by Levi-Civita and Synge [701–705], this
equation describes the evolution of theinfinitesimalseparation between two
nearby free-falling test particles. Explicitly, it reads
D2yµ
dτ 2 =−R µ
νρσuνyρuσ,(8.1)
wherey µ describes the infinitesimal separation as a vector andu µ describes
the unit-normalized four-velocity.
The generalization of the GDE to the case offiniteseparations has been
a topic of research in the literature [706–722]. Here, the objective is to find
corrections to the GDE perturbatively in the orders of the separation vector
y, covariantly defined as a tangent to the geodesic segment joining the two
particles. Namely, the right-hand side of Eq. (8.1) is augmented with terms
involving nonlinear powers ofy, coupled to the Riemann tensorR µνρσ and its
derivatives. Physically, this captures the higher-order tidal effects due to the
non-infinitesimal size ofy.
The higher-order extensions of the GDE have found fruitful physical applica-
tions [718, 722]: gravitational wave detectors, astrophysical jets, measurement

===== PAGE 239 =====
217
of spacetime curvature, and an analytical treatment of eccentric relativistic
orbits [710–717, 723–731].
The explicit GDEs throughO(y 3) andO(y 4) were obtained by works [706–
709] in the 1970s and Vines [718] in 2014, respectively. Moreover, Vines [718]
also provided formulae for the all-orders extension of the GDE and its La-
grangian formulation, though in terms of tensor expressions that are not fully
expanded out in terms of Riemann curvature and its derivatives. To find the
explicit equation or Lagrangian at an order, one has to solve a group of in-
terrelated recursion relations in Appendix 3 of Ref. [718], which arise in the
context of various and intricate identities of the Synge bitensor formalism
[626, 732–734].
In this work, we intend to revisit this problem from a different framework.
The achievements are the following. Firstly, we develop an alternative to
the Synge formalism in which the relevant tensor expressions are computed
from a covariant calculus of differential forms. Secondly, we provide the exact
all-orders formula for the so-called Jacobi propagators [229, 735–737], fully ex-
panded out in terms of Riemann tensor and its derivatives. Our exact expres-
sions are infinite sums whose coefficients are products of binomial coefficients,
showing agreements with Vines [718]. Consequently, we provide the explicit
GDE and its Lagrangian up toO(y 10)
The key idea of our approach is to formulate geodesic deviation as an initial-
value problem like in works [738–743]. Namely, we specify anin-inboundary
condition for the geodesic: pointxand a tangent vectoryatx. This is to be
contrasted with the Synge bitensor formalism [626, 732–734] where a geodesic
segment is characterized by its two endpointsxandzas anin-outboundary
condition.
8.2 Geodesic Deviation in Tangent Bundle
Let (M,g) be ad-dimensional real-analytic manifold with local coordinates
xµ and metricg µν, which we call spacetime. Its tangent bundle,TM, can be
viewed as a 2d-dimensional manifold with local coordinates (x µ,yµ), based on
its local trivialization by the coordinate vector fields.
Coordinate transformations ofTMare restricted in the form (x µ,yµ)7→
(fµ(x),f µ,ν(x)yν), wheref µ(x) describes a set of real-analytic functions in a
local patch ofM. Importantly, the notion of covariance inTMis defined with

===== PAGE 240 =====
218
[t]
Figure 8.1: A geodesic segment joins two spacetime pointsx(observer) andz(test
particle). The tangent vectors are respectively denoted asy µ andy µ′
, which are
normalized such thatxandzare related by unit-time geodesic flows.
respect to these coordinate transformations.
In particular, the following vector field inTMisinvariantunder such co-
ordinate transformations:
N=y µ ∂
∂xµ−Γµ
ρσ(x)yρyσ ∂
∂yµ,(8.2)
where Γµρσ(x) denote the Christoffel symbols. This can be easily seen by
considering its interior products with a one-form basis inTMthat transform
covariantly:
ιNdxµ =y µ, ι NDyµ = 0.(8.3)
Here,Ddenotes the covariant exterior derivative:Dy µ =dy µ + Γµνρ(x)yνdxρ.
In light of its invariance,Ndefines a structure characteristic of the tangent
bundle. In the mathematical jargon, it describes a horizontal vector field due
to the Ehresmann notion of a connection [381, 744].
Crucially,Nholds the very significance as thegenerator of geodesic devia-
tion. To see this, consider the first-order formulation of the geodesic equation:
d
dηXµ(η) =Y µ(η),
d
dηYµ(η) =−Γ µ
ρσ(X(η))Y ρ(η)Y σ(η).
(8.4)
Evidently, the explicit components ofNin Eq. (8.2) encode Eq. (8.4). This
implies that the power series solution of Eq. (8.4) is given byX µ(η) = eηNxµ
andY µ(η) = eηNyµ for initial conditionsX µ(0) =x µ andY µ(0) =y µ, where
Nis understood as a differential operator. Namely, the time-ηflow ofNsolves
the geodesic equation.

===== PAGE 241 =====
219
In particular, consider the unit-time flow:
Xµ(1) = eNxµ, Y µ(1) = eNyµ.(8.5)
Borrowing the notation in the Synge bitensor formalism [626, 732–734], we
rephrase Eq. (8.5) as the following:
zµ′
=δ µ′
µ (eNxµ), y µ′
=δ µ′
µ (eNyµ),(8.6)
whereδ µ′
µ is Kronecker delta. Namely, we assign unprimed and primed in-
dices to objects at the original (x) and deviated (z) points, respectively. The
rationale might be that indices represent the behavior under coordinate trans-
formations, and tensors atxandztransform with different Jacobian factors.
Fig. 8.1 provides a spacetime picture that visualizes Eq. (8.6).
The unit-time flow ofNcan be regarded as a diffeomorphism inTM. By
the very definition of the Lie derivative, the pullback of a tensorαinTM
is given by e£Nα. Practically, this pullback replaces everyxwith eNxandy
with eNy. For instance, the following identity holds:
dzµ′
=δ µ′
µ (e£Ndxµ).(8.7)
Notably, for differential forms, Lie derivatives are neatly computed by using
the Cartan magic formula:
£N =dι N +ιNd.(8.8)
We leave it as an exercise to check Eq. (8.7) at each order inyby using Eq. (8.8).
8.3 Geodesic Deviation in A Direct Sum Bundle
For pedagogical reasons, let us also consider a slight generalization of the
above construction. Suppose the direct sum of the tangent and cotangent
bundles:P=TM⊕T ∗M, where local coordinates are (xµ,yµ,pµ). Eventually,
this 3d-dimensional space will serve as our geometrical arena for a first-order
formulation of GDE. In this larger bundle, the vector fieldNis uniquely
defined byι Ndxµ =y µ,ι NDyµ = 0, andι NDpµ = 0.
It is not difficult to see that thisNis the generator of geodesic deviation
and parallel transport. In particular, consider the analog of the first-order
differential equations in Eq. (8.4) to find thatp µ′ = (eNpµ)δµµ′ is the covector

===== PAGE 242 =====
220
pµ parallel-transported to the pointzalong the geodesic; see Fig. 8.1. This
finding is succinctly stated as
pµ′ =p µWµ
µ′,(8.9)
whereW µµ′ denotes theparallel propagator: the Wilson line of the Levi-Civita
connection computed about the geodesic betweenxandz. Namely,p µ 7→
pµ′ =p µWµµ′ describes the isomorphism between the cotangent spaces atx
andzfacilitated by the geodesic parallel transport.
Again, (x,y,p)7→ eηN (x,y,p) describes the time-ηflow inPgenerated
by the vector fieldN. Due to this geometrical interpretation, the unit-time
geodesic deviation and parallel transport is given by the exponentiated Lie
derivative e£N .
For a concrete example, consider a one-formpµdxµ, i.e., the trivial extension
of the cotangent bundle’s tautological one-form toP. Then we have
pµ′dzµ′
= e£N (pµdxµ),(8.10)
the right-hand side of which can be computed order-by-order by series-expanding
e£N . We leave it as an exercise to carry out this computation and check con-
sistency between the left-hand and right-hand sides.
8.4 Covariant Lie Derivative and Dressing Identity
The computations utilizing£ N, however, are not very efficient. One encoun-
ters unoccupied connection coefficients arising from the components ofN, so
covariance at the original pointxis not manifested in intermediate steps.
In this light, we introduce a shorthand notation,
£D
N =Dι N +ιND,(8.11)
defined on any tensor-valued differential form. This describes a covariantized
analog of the Lie derivative; more information might be found in Refs. [745–
747].
Crucially, sincep µdxµ is a scalar-valued one-form carrying no free indices,
usingDinstead ofdmakes no difference. Hence we can equivalently use
exp(£D
N) in Eq. (8.10). It is a privilege of exponentiated operators that they
are distributable as
e£D
N (pµdxµ) = (e£D
Npµ)(e£D
Ndxµ) =p µ(e£D
Ndxµ),(8.12)

===== PAGE 243 =====
221
where the last equality follows from£ D
Npµ =ι NDpµ = 0. Therefore, from
Eqs. (8.9), (8.10), and (8.12), it follows that
Wµ
µ′dzµ′
= e£D
Ndxµ.(8.13)
In the same way, it can be shown that the exp(£ D
N) of a tensor-valued
differential form computes its value at the deviated point, followed by the
parallel-transportation back to the original point via dressing by the Wilson
lines. This fact will be referred to as thedressing identity.

directly obtained by implementing our formalism simply in the tangent bundle
TM, instead of employing the extended bundleP.

8.8 Zero-Torsion Identities
One may notice that the behavior of ´Xµνρ and ´Yµνρ in Eq. (8.31) is sug-
gestive of connection coefficients. In fact, they arise from the conjugation

8.9 Summary and Outlook
In this work, we revisited the problem of finding the all-orders-exact GDE for
finite separations by formulating geodesic deviation and transport as a flow
along a vector field in tangent bundle. The technique of covariant Lie derivative
then systematically defines and generates various bitensors with the parallel
propagators peeled off, directly producing manifestly covariantized expressions
at the original point. This achieves an in-in formalism for geodesic deviation
that serves as an alternative to the Synge calculus.

===== PAGE 250 =====
228
The explicit outcomes are the all-orders formula for the Jacobi propagators
in Eq. (8.21) as well as the second-order GDE and its Lagrangian obtained up
toO(y 10).
Our tangent-bundle formalism for geodesic deviation is a versatile tool and
can find applications in various areas of physics, besides classical gravitation:
covariant worldline perturbation theory [750–764], quantum field theory [282],
and sigma models [391, 764–767], for instance. Sec. 8.C implements our for-
malism for gauge-covariant translations in nonabelian gauge theories.
Furthermore, we realize that our innocuous attempt to remaster the fun-
damental subject of all-orders-in-deviation GDE surprisingly connects to a
persistent and seemingly disparate problem in modern gravitational physics:
finding the all-orders-in-spin EoM for the Kerr black hole in its effective point-
particle description [93, 94, 97, 98, 207–209, 226]. By implementing a probe
counterpart of the Newman-Janis algorithm [202], we have found that the
all-orders GDE for an imaginary deviation can deduce a part of the black
hole’s all-orders-in-spin EoM, yielding a consistent nonlinear completion of
the Mathisson-Papapetrou-Dixon [227–229] equations. The details have been
given in Refs. [768–770].

8.C Gauge-Covariant Translations in Nonabelian Gauge Theory
It should be remarked that our framework applies to not only gravity but
also nonabelian gauge theories. Suppose a nonabelian gauge theory in ad-

which is locally isomorphic toR 2d×C N. Local trivialization equipsPwith

C h a p t e r 9
PROBE NEWMAN-JANIS ALGORITHM
We implement a probe counterpart of NJA, which Wick rotates the all-orders
geodesic deviation equation into a part of exact spinning-particle EoM. Conse-
quently, the gravitational dynamics of the Kerr black hole in its point-particle
effective theory is completely constrained in the SD sector for a hidden symme-
try, implying the spin exponentiation of same-helicity gravitational Compton
amplitudes to all multiplicities.
This section reproduces the contents of Ref. [768], J.-H. Kim, “Note on the Kerr
spinning-particle equations of motion,”arXiv:2512.23697 [gr-qc].
9.1 Introduction
Suppose a free-falling probe particle in space, followed by an observer. Their
separation is famously described by the geodesic deviation equation. Yet,
typical textbooks describe this equation only in the limit of infinitesimal sep-
aration. The exact equation valid to all orders inseparationandcurvature
was investigated by Vines [718]. Recently, we have revisited this topic from
an alternative formalism [695].
It seems that this foundational subject of geodesic deviation is intimately
tied to a pressing problem in contemporary gravitational physics: the exact
dynamics of the Kerr black hole as a probe object.
Suppose a Kerr black hole in the presence of mild gravitational perturba-
tions; it may travel through macroscopic gravitational waves or constitute an
inspiral-phase binary system. It is well-established [92–97] that the black hole
can be treated as a relativistic spinning point particle in theeffective theory
sense, provided separation of scales. In this effective description, the persistent
problem has been determining the exact EoM for the Kerr black hole in the
probe limit, to all orders in the so-calledspin length(spin per mass, encoding
the ring radius) andcurvature. At the linear order in curvature, the EoM have
been completely fixed to all orders in spin length [226, 673]. Frustratingly, am-
biguities arise from the very quadratic order in curvature: in layman’s terms,
physicists in this 21 st century do not know the “ ⃗F=m⃗ a” of spinning black

===== PAGE 260 =====
238
holes. This agony has gained a considerable attention lately and has been
scrutinized in terms of scattering amplitudes [98, 206–225].
The widespread expectation is that the Kerr black hole will exhibit the
simplestEoM among all massive spinning objects in four dimensions, in light
of its simplicity uncovered in a modern analysis [98, 206–209] that traces back
to the NJA [202]. The NJA, introduced by the very article “Note on the Kerr
Spinning-Particle Metric” in 1965 [202], is a method that derives the Kerr
solution by displacing the Schwarzschild solution along a complexified direction
in a sense. Crucially, this reinterprets spin as an imaginary deviation [252, 253].
Due to Ref. [164], the NJA now holds the status of a rigorous derivation of the
Kerr solution with a definite origin.
This article shows that a unique answer exists for the simplest spinning-
particle EoM to all orders in spin and curvature, in SD spacetimes. This Wick
rotates thedeviation vectorin the exact geodesic deviation equation to the
spin length pseudovectorin exact spinning-particle EoM, as an incarnation of
NJA at the probe level.
Our proposal is distinguished in terms of a hidden symmetry in the presence
of Killing-Yano tensors. In perturbation theory, it implies the property known
as spin exponentiation [98, 206–212] at all graviton multiplicities.

The free-fall EoM of a scalar probe is given in the first-order formulation as

theory.

Let us pause for a second to clarify the interpretations. The Wick rotation
in Eq. (9.5) implements the idea of NJA at the level of probe EoM, soy µ is
now interpreted as the spin length pseudovector. Its definition reads
yµ =−∗S µνpν/p2,(9.7)

Consequently, all ofx µ,p µ, andy µ had to be interpreted as dynamical
variables of a spinning particle, requiring a total of 12 equations. The addi-
tional stipulation in Eq. (9.6c) was exactly for this purpose. Physically, such
a synchronization of orbital (pµ) and spin (yµ) precessions will encode a spin-
ning/complexified analog of equivalence principle proposed in Ref. [672].
Finally, we enter the last step of our procedure. The 12 equations in
Eqs. (9.6a), (9.6b), and (9.6c) together define well-posed spinning-particle EoM,
only if the variablesx µ,pµ,yµ are complexified. This is because the Wick ro-
tation in Eq. (9.5) induces imaginary units inside the tensorsα µν,β µν, Ωµν.
Therefore, Eq. (9.6) describes the EoM of a spinning particle incomplexified
spacetime.
Thus, we propose that each imaginary unit in Eq. (9.6) is eliminated by
trading off with a Hodge star∗on a Riemann tensor, which is justified if the
complexified spacetime is taken asSD:i7→∗viaiR µνρσ =∗R µνρσ. In this

===== PAGE 263 =====

manner, Eq. (9.6) is uplifted to a set of completely real equations that dictates
the time evolution ofx µ,pµ,yµ as real variables. Details will be elaborated
later.
Parity property ofy µ implies that the linear-in-curvature part of Eq. (9.6)
reproduces the unity [202, 203, 256] of spin-induced multipole moments of the
Kerr black hole, if uplifted in this precise fashion. This provides a justifica-
tion. Intuitively, we have turned the localized orbital momentum in geodesic
deviation (yielding unity of mass multipoles) into a spin angular momentum.
In sum, we have proposed a probe counterpart of NJA that uniquely derives
a well-posed instance of spinning-particle EoM inheaven, which will then be
readily uplifted toearth. Here, we have used technical terminologies coined by
Newman [258, 328] and Pleba´ nski [314, 315]:
Heaven=SD Spacetime,
Earth=Real Spacetime.
(9.8)
To clarify, heaven is a complex-analytic four-manifold with a holomorphic
metric, while earth is a real-analytic four-manifold with a pseudo-Riemannian
metric.
Remarkably, the following hold for Eq. (9.6) in heaven:
(a) The linear-in-curvature part of Eq. (9.6) describes the established
linear coupling for Kerr.
(b) The dynamics due to Eq. (9.6) features exact (all-orders) conserved
quantities in the presence of Killing vectors and Killing-Yano tensors.
(c) Eq. (9.6) admits a Lagrangian formulation.
In these senses, Eq. (9.6) is an exact and consistent nonlinear completion of
the linearized Kerr spinning-particle EoM, characterized by hidden symmetry.
Hence we propose that Eq. (9.6) istheexact Kerr spinning-particle EoM in SD
spacetimes. Below, we refer to Eq. (9.6) as theheavenly Kerr spinning-particle
EoM.

system. Previous works have truncated the probe’s spin order [233, 790–792]
or NJ (Newman-Janis) shifted only the background [266, 267].
To summarize, we have shown that exact symmetries arise for our proposed
Kerr spinning particle by the very structure of the probe NJA. The conserved
quantities of the spinning probe can arise as direct NJ shifts of the conserved
quantities of the scalar probe. For the explicit example of physical relevance,
we have found a sufficient number of functionally independent conserved quan-
tities, establishing superintegrability.

9.7 Spin Exponentiation at All Multiplicities
Finally, let us explore all orders in perturbation theory:
(d) Positive-helicity graviton Compton amplitudes due to the action in
Eq. (9.18) exhibit spin exponentiation at all multiplicities.
No physical explanation has existed for this property to the best of our knowl-
edge, although its consistency at the amplitudes level has been known [210–
212]. In our case, the structure of the probe NJA is highly constrained for the
hidden symmetries in the SD sector, whose implication is spin exponentiation
at all multiplicities.
To prove proposition (d), we expand the gravitational fields around Minkowski
background, yet after switching to the tetrad formalism. In tetrad formalism,
the constraints and Hamiltonian (put inCin Eq. (9.18)) take the same form as

with any such real uplift, as scattering amplitudes are (worldline) field redefi-

Eventually, we elaborate on the uplifting procedure, which provides the ex-
tension to generic backgrounds withboth SD and ASD modes. For instance,
recallα µν in Eq. (9.4) and suppose the Wick rotation in Eq. (9.5) is applied.
On the support of SDity, each imaginary unitiis absorbed to a Riemann

9.10 Interpretation
The NJA had been in a mysterious status for decades. Despite initial criticisms
[243, 634], constant attempt was made for explanations [202, 242, 243, 246–249,
330, 611]. Especially, Newman himself envisioned a fundamental unification
of spin and spacetime into a complex geometry, in which the spinning particle
truly becomes a scalar particle [252–254, 254–259, 329, 330]. This idea was
not only pursued at the level of sourced fields but also at the level of probe
particles: “complex center of mass.” An attempt for EoM appears in Ref. [259].
This chapter vividly realizes Newman’s dream in terms of explicit and rig-
orous frameworks of geodesic deviation [695] and adapted complex structure
[743, 776–782]. The holomorphic worldlinez µ′
is Newman’s complex center
of mass, realized in fully nonlinear gravity. However, our analysis on earthly
deformations clarifies that Newman’s proposal of the Kerr spinning particle
being equivalent to a scalar particle is valid only in the SD sector. To reiter-
ate, the probe NJA pinpoints a unique dynamicswithinheaven. This point is
made further evident in Chap. X, where an explicit ASD group of terms ruins
the equivalence between Eqs. (9.18) and (9.20) in earth.
A similar realization has been critical in grasping the full content of the
NJA [164]. The SD limit of the Kerr-Taub-NUT solution is diffeomorphic to
the SD Taub-NUT solution [260]. In Kerr-Schild coordinates [163, 164], this
diffeomorphism is just a translation by +i⃗ a. Crucially, however, the non-SD
Kerr solution describestwocenters at±i⃗ ain the complexified description: a
pair of SDand ASDTaub-NUT solutions [164]. Thus, the equivalence between
Kerr and a pointlike object is lost beyond the SD sector.
Based on this repertoire that applies for both our probe NJA and the NJA,
it seems that a natural interpretation for the holomorphic worldlinez µ′
in
Eq. (9.3a) is the worldline of the ASD Taub-NUT instanton constituting the
Kerr black hole. Surely, the anti-holomorphic worldline could have also been
obtained by geodesically deviating in the direction of−iy µ, in which case the
SD Taub-NUT instanton is reached.
5A similar phenomenon had occurred in the electromagnetic analog [795].

===== PAGE 271 =====
249
This interpretation is nicely consistent with spin exponentiation, disclosing
an underlying physical picture: spin exponentiation is the helicity selection
rule [261, 263, 264] of Taub-NUT instantons. It also provides a physical in-
terpretation for the worldsheet structure speculated in Ref. [226]: the Misner
string (gravitational Dirac string) as a flux tube of NUT charge joining the
SD and ASD instantons.
Due to the nonlinearity of gravity inherent in Eq. (9.3a), there is a difficulty
in simultaneously manifesting the nonlinear NJ shifts (all-multiplicity spin ex-
ponentiations, spin resummations) for both SD (positive-helicity) and ASD
(negative-helicity) sectors. Still, we shall make maximum use of the super-
integrability of the SD sector shown in Eq. (9.17) by perturbing around the
holomorphic variablezµ′
: agoogly[347–350] approach to Kerr spinning-particle
effective theory.
9.11 Conclusion
We proposed the probe counterpart of NJA to uniquely pinpoint a remarkable
instance of a massive spinning particle in SD spacetimes, which exhibits exact
symmetries in the presence of Killing vectors and Killing-Yano tensors. It
is shown that this distinguished point in the effective theory landscape of
all massive spinning particles enjoys the privileges of superintegrability in SD
Taub-NUT background and spin exponentiation at every graviton multiplicity.
It is also determined that the uplift to real spacetimes is ambiguous precisely
by mixed-chirality deformations, corresponding to contact deformations for
Compton amplitudes.
This work provides a partial answer to the persistent question, “What prin-
ciple in the effective theory characterizes Kerr among all massive spinning
objects?” Previously, the speculations had described asimplicityof linearized
interactions [98, 206–209] or its continuation [210–212], apatternsuggested
at low orders [213, 214], or thegauge redundancyin the higher-spin formal-
ism [217, 218]. However, we identified an exact hiddensymmetryembedded
in nonlinear interactions, thus demonstrating that a physical principle can
directly address the question.

===== PAGE 272 =====
250
C h a p t e r 10
THE KERR EFFECTIVE ACTION
Contents of this chapter are adapted from Ref. [769], J.-H. Kim, “The Kerr two-
twistor particle,”arXiv:2602.19495 [gr-qc].
10.1 Introduction
The twistor particle program [377, 796–808] was a movement in the ’70s to
’80s that applied twistor theory to particle physics. In particular, massive
particles were implemented as systems of two or more twistors, whose internal
symmetries were associated with color or flavor.
In a modern reboot of this program [673, 794], the two-twistor implemen-
tation is adopted such that theSU(2) internal symmetry describes the mas-
sive little group instead, in accordance with Ref. [206]. This revival has also
expanded the scope from particle physics to astrophysics, based on the point-
particle effective theory of macroscopic bodies [92–97] such as neutron stars
or black holes.
The two-twistor system should be able to realize interacting particles; see
Refs. [673, 802, 809–812] for previous attempts. The problem of direct rele-
vance to the current PM gravity community is to implement the Kerr black
hole coupled to curved spacetime, capturing all its 2 ℓ-pole moments forℓ=
0,1,2,···[97, 176, 202, 203, 207–209, 256] as well as further nonlinear-in-
curvature couplings.
In this chapter, we describe how an attempt toward curved massive twistor
theory had resulted in a candidate effective point-particle Lagrangian for the
Kerr black hole to all orders in spin and curvature. This construction was
originally obtained in 2022 by the present author.
This derivation implements an intrinsic feature of twistor particle theory
that spin is literally an imaginary deviation in terms of complexified incidence
relation [252, 813, 814]. As a result, the NJA [202] is realized for dynamical
particles. A differential-geometric formulation arises in terms of the generator
of geodesic deviation,N, and an almost-complex structure,J.

===== PAGE 273 =====
251

10.2.2 Twistor Magic
A remarkable feature of the twistor particle framework is that an imaginary
shift of the two-twistor’s co-incident position derives a spinning particle from
a scalar particle.

(R4,η) is established by the incidence relation [611, 814], the massive adapta-

10.2.3 Spinspacetime
The complexified Minkowski space in this context is dubbed spinspacetime
[672]:
bM ∼= TM.(10.10)
As a real manifold, spinspacetime is the tangent bundleTMof the space-
timeM, with base coordinatesx ˙ααand fiber coordinatesy ˙αα. As a complex
manifold, spinspacetime is equipped with complex coordinates
z ˙αα =x ˙αα+iy ˙αα,(10.11)
which are holomorphic due to the almost-complex structuredx ˙αα7→dy ˙αα,
dy ˙αα7→−dx ˙αα. (In this chapter, we wish to formulate complex structures as
isomorphisms of the cotangent bundle rather than of the tangent bundle.)
Smooth tensor fields on spacetime are analytically continued to smooth ten-
sor fields on spinspacetime. Their Cauchy-Riemann flow arises by the expo-
nentiated Lie derivative exp(i£N), whereN∈Γ(TM):
N=y ˙αα ∂
∂x ˙αα.(10.12)
In particular, the complexified metric isbη= exp(i£ N)η.
As explicated in Refs. [252, 672], the physical origin of Eq. (10.11) is a Hodge
duality in the decomposition of angular momentum for massive particles,
j= (x∧p) +∗(y∧p).(10.13)
The SD part of Eq. (10.13) is the SD part of (z∧p), as∗is sent to +i. The
complexification in Eq. (10.7) precisely arises in this way viaj ˙α˙β in Eq. (10.3).
10.2.4 Correspondence Space
In massless twistor theory, the incidence relation is mathematically formalized
as the double fibration. Let us achieve its massive equivalent.
LetS 2bMbe a trivialGL(2,C) bundle over bM. Fig. 10.1 concerns two maps
φ: (z ˙αα;λαI)7→(λ αI,iz ˙ααλαI) andπ 2 : (z ˙αα;λαI)7→z ˙ααfrom it. The first
is an invertible diffeomorphism. The second is the bundle projection. This
provides a mathematical formalization of the massive incidence relation in
Eq. (10.7), establishing the relation between massive twistor spaceMTand
spinspacetime bM.

10.3 Earth
The desire of this chapter is to implement the twistor magic in curved back-
grounds. However, we do not have a definition of “curved massive twistor
space” yet.
Recall the scaffolding of curved twistor theory [309, 310, 313] (cf. App. A.3).
One may start by discussing the geometry of the correspondence space within
the conventional mathematical languages of general relativity. Then the “rad-
ical” twistor-space picture will arise afterwards via the self-duality condition
as integrability alongα-surfaces.
We shall follow the same approach. First of all, we have to realize the
curved massive correspondence spaceKas the typical phase space of a mas-
sive spinning particle in textbook general relativity. After establishing this
“conservative” description, we may then envision how a notion of curved mas-
sive twistor spaceMTcould emerge.

10.3.1 Curved Correspondence Space
In textbook general relativity, spacetime (M,g) is a real-analytic pseudo-
Riemannian four-manifold. Local trivialization ofTMdescribes the frame
Em =E µm(x)∂µ and coframee m =e mµ(x)dxµ such that⟨e m,En⟩=δ mn.
Here,m,n,···= 0,1,2,3 are local Lorentz indices whileµ,ν,···= 0,1,2,3
are spacetime indices.
The decompositionTM ∼= (S+⊗S−)Minto SD and ASD spinor bundles
describes that a local Lorentz index is a pair of spinor indices asem↔e ˙αα. The
Levi-Civita connection∇ofgsplits toSL(2,C)-valued connections ¯γandγon
S±M. The structure equations are 0 =De ˙αα=de ˙αα−¯γ˙α˙β∧e

andRαβ =dγ αβ +γαγ∧γγβ, whereDdenotes the covariant exterior derivative

Physically, the degrees of freedom of a massive spinning particle in (M,g)
are described byx µ,y m, andλ αI. Here, the index form declares the behaviors
under coordinate and local Lorentz transformations. The position variablex µ
is supposed to describe coordinates onM. The spin length pseudovectory m
and the spin frameλ αI are supposed to be local Lorentz degrees of freedom.
Therefore, the curved massive correspondence space should be defined as
the direct sum bundle

10.4 Heaven
10.4.1 Heavenly Portal to Curved Spinspacetime
Eq. (10.27) and its explicit evaluation in Eq. (10.29) provide a manifestly co-
variant, gauge-invariant, and reparametrization-invariant construction of the
Kerr action in generic, real spacetimes. It is based on two invariant geometric
structures in the spinning-particle phase space:NandJ.
However, it is based on the typical languages of general relativity. Appar-
ently, the spinspacetime and massive twistor descriptions are lost. This means
that the curved analogs of the left part of the diagram in Fig. 10.1 are yet to
be constructed.
The first step toward the “radical” reformulations is to identify the map
γ. It can be discovered by traveling to SD spacetimes, i.e.,heavenin the
terminologies of Newman [258, 328] and Pleba´ nski [314, 315].
The spacetime curvatureR mn splits to SD and ASD parts as ¯R ˙α˙β and
Rαβ. Consider the (formal) complexified limit in which the latter vanishes,
so⋆R mn = +iRmn. Then Eq. (10.28) equals

withitimes the Gilbertian dipolep mDym, and upon this very mechanism
spin becomesitimes deviation—which is the gist of the NJA, in fact [164].
Hence, physically speaking, the almost-complex structureJhas implemented
a duality between Gilbertian and Amp` erian multipoles. This observation
can be rephrased as electric-magnetic or Hodge duality; recall the discussions
around Eqs. (10.16) and (10.17).

10.4.2 Curved Spinspacetime
As shown in Sec. 10.A, spinspacetime exists for any real-analytic spacetime.
To expedite our discussion, however, we may want to directly jump to a com-

Eq. (10.38) provides a chiral formulation of the Kerr particle, treatingλ αI

freedomy m′

Eq. (10.39) concretely verifies the spinning, complexified analog of equiva-

fields, which the current author proposed through works [672, 673]: the left-
handed spin frameλαI is frozen, i.e., does not precess at all, in SD backgrounds.
When transcribed to the defining chart (covariantized atx µ in spacetime),
Eq. (10.39) yields an all-orders-in-spin extension of Mathisson-Papapetrou-
Dixon equations withC ℓ = 1 for allℓ. Moreover, Eq. (10.39) implies exact
hidden symmetries in the presence of Killing and Killing-Yano tensors by
dynamically NJ shifting the conserved quantities of the Schwarzschild probe
[768].

In massless curved twistor theory, the celebrated nonlinear graviton con-
struction of Penrose [313] formulates SD gravity as deformations of the com-
plex structure in twistor space. This describes deformations of the twistor
lines in terms of the deformed incidence relation.
Our proposal above envisions curved massive twistor theory from a deformed
massive incidence relation relevant to perturbation theory, which is reminiscent
yet not fully equivalent to massless curved twistor theory. It may be also
possible to consider a formulation that follows the ideas of massless curved
twistor theory more closely, in which case the symplectic structure might be
fixed.
10.4.6 Googly Kerr Action
In Eq. (10.29), we have already obtained the Kerr action in generic spacetimes
with both SD and ASD modes, i.e.,earthin the terminologies of Newman
[258, 328] and Pleba´ nski [314, 315].
We then took an excursion to SD backgrounds, i.e.,heaven, to discover
the nonlinear NJ shift in Eq. (10.35). The key punchline is that theNJ shift
manifests in heavenas the localization of the Kerr action on the holomorphic
worldlinez µ′
.
It should be stressed that there is an obstruction in simultaneously mani-
festing the NJ shifts in earth for both SD and ASD sectors. This obstruction is
due to the very nonlinearity of gravity and poses a massive analog of the goo-
gly [347–350] problem. Concretely, it is impossible to write the earthly Kerr
action as a sum of two separate worldline actions localized respectively atz µ′
and ˜zµ′′
, since there exist nonlinear mixing between SD and ASD curvatures.

===== PAGE 289 =====
267
See also Sec. 10.B, where the same problem is found for the case of non-
abelian gauge theory.
The best we can do is to perturb away from the SD sector, which is a typical
twistor-theorist move. Assuming the complexified setup, the Kerr symplectic
potential in Eq. (10.27) can be written as

manifestly guaranteed in the SD sector to all graviton multiplicities [768], on
top of which the ASD perturbations are systematically added on.

10.5 Interpretation
In Eq. (10.48), ( e−2i£N−1)/(−2i£N) =
R 1
0 dη e−2iη£N inserts the NJ shifts
ofθ −
G2A along the geodesic segment joining the two pointszand ˜z. Hence
Eq. (10.48) states that the Kerr black hole describes a dangling open string
attached to a point mass atz. This string is charged under ASD gravitons
(dθ−
G2A =pmR−mnyn) but is invisible to SD fields as remarked earlier. In fact,
recalling our earlier discussions on Gilbertian and Amp` erian dipoles, it pre-
cisely describes the ASD combination of electric and magnetic mass dipole
moments. Thus, we identify its interpretation as an ASD Misner string.
Similarly, it can be seen that Eq. (10.27) describes two point masses joined
by a thin gravitomagnetic flux tube: an ordinary Misner string 4. The two
pictures, googly and real, are equivalent via identifying a pair of opposite-sign
masses as a line defect of electric mass dipole.
The above analysis elicits a provocative idea that the Kerr black hole de-
scribes a system of SD and ASD dyons. At least in the effective theory of
Kerr black holes, Eqs. (10.34) and (10.48) show that this statement holds true
as the physical origin of the NJ shift. The question is whether it holds in the
ultraviolet theory, i.e., general relativity, as a nonperturbative fact.
The persistent endeavor of the current author around this question had
resulted in works [163, 164], which established that the Kerr metric represents
the exact nonlinear superposition of SD and ASD Taub-NUT solutions as a
holomorphic saddle, from which the NJA [202] is faithfully reproduced.
The worldlinesz µ′
and ˜zµ′′
are the infrared avatars of the Taub-NUT instan-
tons. The worldline actions due to Eqs. (10.27) and (10.48) insert the Misner
string along the geodesic between the two instantons. As long as the geodesic-
ity of the Misner string (or locality in worldline proper time) can be assumed,
Eq. (10.27), equivalently Eq. (10.48), defines the unique answer for the effective
action that describes a dynamical pair of SD and ASD Taub-NUT instantons.
Regarding this point, it will be helpful to investigate if the Misner string serves
as a genuinely topological surface defect in dynamical, full (non-SD) gravity
(cf. Sec. 3.5), in which case the Misner worldsheet can be realized in any shape
to predict the same physical observables.
4This string structure was noticed in Ref. [226], but the physical interpretations and
googly formulation are not provided there. Ref. [226] also does not provide explicit expres-
sions such as Eqs. (10.29) and (10.48) that expand out the action fully in terms of covariant
worldline operators.

===== PAGE 291 =====
269
10.6 Conclusions
In this chapter, we derived an explicit all-orders worldline effective action for
the Kerr black hole by generalizing an intrinsic feature of twistor particle
theory in curved spacetime. This implements the NJA at the level of test-
particle actions, in terms of differential-geometric elementsNandJ. Nonlin-
ear, dynamical NJ shift manifests in SD backgrounds as localizations on the
holomorphic worldline. The full gravitational interactions are systematically
understood by perturbing around the SD sector. Clear physical interpreta-
tions are given and are substantiated by the ultraviolet description of the Kerr
black hole.
This chapter is written in the geometrical language and wishes to deliver
itself to the twistor theory community as well. This chapter attempts to
make a small yet ambitious step towards “curved massive twistor theory,”
which shall exist in light of the histories of massless twistor theory and twistor
particle program. Some tentative identifications and definitions are given for
the deformed massive incidence relation and curved massive twistor space,
based on the particular case of the Kerr black hole as a massive twistor.
10.A Spinspacetime from Adapted Complex Structure
This appendix elaborates on the definition of spinspacetime for real spacetime.
In Eq. (10.10), we discussed two perspectives toward spinspacetime: real
and complex manifolds. In the case of curved real-analytic spacetime (M,g),
however, the latter picture is seemingly lost. The formula in Eq. (10.11) be-
comes utterly nonsensical, sincex µ arecoordinateswhiley µ are components
of avector. This represents a clash between two necessary features of “curved
spinspacetime”: general covariance and holomorphy.
Luckily, the mathematical framework known as adapted complex structure
[743, 776–782] provides a nice resolution, showing that a sufficiently small
neighborhood of the zero section of the tangent bundleTMof a real-analytic
(M,g) can be embedded into a complex manifold cMvia complexifying the
exponential map (geodesic flow):
Πi :T ′M → cM.(10.49)
Here, we have supposed a sufficiently narrow neighborhoodT ′Mof the zero
section inTM.

===== PAGE 292 =====
270

bα∈Ωp(T′M) α∈Ω p(M)
Πi
∗ a.c.
Figure 10.5: The “central dogma” of adapted complex structure.
The idea is simple. The time-ηexponential map sends the pair (x,y)∈T ′M
to the point inMwhose coordinates arew µ(x,y;η) =x µ+ηyµ−η2
2
Γµρσ(x)yρyσ+
O(y3). This is a power series solution which converges inT ′M. Upon a real-
analytic coordinate transformationx µ7→f µ(x) onM, the coordinates of the
geodesically deviated point transform asw µ(x,y;η)7→f µ(w(x,y;η)) for any
η, by construction.
By analytic continuation,z µ(x,y) :=w µ(x,y;i) is well-defined withinT ′M,
whose explicit formula is exactly Eq. (10.35). By analyticity, the coordinate
transformation onMinducesz µ(x,y)7→f Cµ(z(x,y)), wheref C is the analytic
continuation off. This shows thatz µ(x,y) defines a holomorphic coordinate
chart onT ′M. Therefore,T ′Mbecomes a complex manifold.
Physically speaking, this construction ensures that holomorphy is an observer-
independent notion. This is important since holomorphy will be linked with
self-duality by the NJ shift.
Now let us unravel the precise mathematical details. The time-ηexponential
map can be formalized as
Πη =π◦Φ η :T ′M→M,(10.50)
where Φη :T ′M→TMdenotes the time-ηflow by the horizontal vector field
N∈Γ(TM) such thatι Ndxµ =y µ, andπ:TM→Mis the bundle projec-
tion. The pullback Πη
∗ maps analytic functions onMto analytic functions on
T′M, the explicit power series solutions for which arise by the exponentiated
Lie derivative eη£N .
By analytic continuation toη∈C, one defines Π i
∗ as a map from real-
analytic functions onMto complex-analytic functions onT ′M, the applica-
tion of which on coordinate functions establishes the map Π i in Eq. (10.49)
such thatM, as the zero section ofTM, is embedded in the complex-analytic
manifold cM=M C as a totally real submanifold of maximal dimension
[743, 817, 818].
The practical use of this formalism is facilitated by the fact that the ex-

Historically, it was Newman who first envisioned the concept of spinspace-

insights in terms of concrete differential-geometric constructions.

C h a p t e r 11
THE KERR-NEWMAN EFFECTIVE ACTION
An all-orders worldline effective action for Kerr-Newman black hole is achieved
in twistor particle theory. Exact hidden symmetries are identified in SD back-
grounds.
This chapter reproduces the contents of Ref. [770], J.-H. Kim, “The Kerr-Newman
two-twistor particle,”arXiv:2603.07537 [gr-qc].
11.1 Introduction
An important topic in the current gravitational physics literature has been
the construction of worldline effective actions [92–97] of spinning black holes.
Recently, Ref. [769] has derived an exact first-order action of the Kerr black
hole and provided its explicit all-orders formula, by using massive twistor
theory [377, 796, 801] and the tangent bundle formalism for geodesic deviation
[695].
Ref. [769]’s derivation of the Kerr action provides the test-particle counter-
part of the NJA [768, 769]. The NJA is a solution-generating technique that
rederived the Kerr metric by performing a complex-geometrical transforma-
tion on the Schwarzschild metric [202]. This trick then facilitated the very
historical derivation of the Kerr-Newman solution [179]. According to a mod-
ern view [164], the NJA encodes that the Kerr solution is diffeomorphic to
a pair of SD and ASD Taub-NUT instantons when taken as a holomorphic
saddle.
The present paper generalizes Ref. [769]’s construction to derive the first-
order action of the Kerr-Newman black hole coupled to background gravita-
tional and electromagnetic fields. Again, the systematic formalism of Ref. [695]
proves instrumental in obtaining explicit formulae for the action. We identify
a Hamiltonian system that can represent the exact, all-orders dynamics of the
Kerr-Newman black hole in its point-particle effective theory.
It is found that the conclusions of Ref. [769] are preserved. Nonlinear NJ shift
manifests in SD backgrounds. A chiral (“googly”) formulation of the Kerr-
Newman action is viable for generic, non-SD external fields. In perturbation

===== PAGE 298 =====
276
theory, the spin exponentiation of same-helicity Compton amplitudes [98, 207,
208, 210–212] is manifested to all graviphoton multiplicities.
We also derive the classical EoM in SD backgrounds. We confirm the com-
plexified, spinning analog of equivalence principle proposed by Ref. [672]. This
implies dynamical hidden symmetries in the presence of Killing-Yano tensors,
generalizing Ref. [768]’s previous result about the Kerr particle.
11.2 Geodesic Deviation in Einstein-Maxwell Geometry
First, we present the new application of Ref. [695]’s tangent bundle formalism
for geodesic deviation.

the covariant exterior derivative due to∇.

tangent bundleTMwith respect to∇. This is the geodesic spray, i.e., the
generator of geodesic deviation inTM.

Eq. (11.1) is readily proven by mathematical induction, based on the recur-

intuition of Ref. [695].
Amusingly, Eq. (11.1) can be quickly seen by a “covariant color-kinematics”
[90] mapping, which views gravity as a gauge theory of the Lorentz group.
Namely, Eq. (11.1) can be deduced from the formula for (ιND)ℓ−1ιNRmn given
in Ref. [695, 769]. By viewing the antisymmetric pair of local Lorentz indices
as the adjoint index of the Lorentz group as a gauge group, (ι ND)ℓ−1ιNRmn
corresponds to (ιND)ℓ−1ιNFa whereF a is a nonabelian field strength. Taking
the abelian limit then reproduces Eq. (11.1).
11.3 The Kerr-Newman Action
Second, we implement the probe counterpart of NJA, in the version of either
Ref. [769] (twistorial, top-down) or Ref. [768] (relatively bottom-up), to derive
the exact effective action of the Kerr-Newman black hole in the presence of
external gravitational and electromagnetic fields.
The gravitational part has been already provided by Ref. [769]: the Kerr two-
twistor particle. In this chapter, we simply need to add the electromagnetic
interaction action. LetA∈Ω 1(M) be the electromagnetic gauge potential,
and letF=dA∈Z 2(M)⊂Ω 2(M) now denote the electromagnetic field
strength.
The “£N sequence” forAis shown in Fig. 11.1. The recipe of Refs. [768, 769]
applies Hodge dualityℓtimes on the 2 ℓ-pole moment term in this sequence.
This determines the electromagnetic interaction symplectic potential. Conse-

The classical EoM due to the symplectic potential in Eq. (11.9) and the first-
class constraintsϕ 0 andϕ 1 are easy to derive by the technique of covariant

Based on the fact that the left-handed spinor bundle is flat for the mo-
ment,Dλ α′I/dτ= 0 explicitly verifies the spinning, complexified analog of
equivalence principle, which was proposed by Ref. [672] as the property char-
acterizing spinning black holes. Namely, there is no ASD component in the
spin precession, in SD backgrounds.
When re-covariantized atx µ in spacetime (i.e., transcribed to the defining
chart ofK), Eq. (11.10) yields an all-orders extension of the Bargmann-Michel-
Telegdi (BMT) [819] and MPD [227–229] equations with all the multipole
coefficients, both electromagnetic and gravitational, set to unity. This is indeed
the desired behavior for the Kerr-Newman particle. Crucial point here is that
the unity of the gravitational multipole coefficients is achieved because we have
utilized geodesic deviation in generating the spin-curvature couplings in the
action.
Similarly, it is also easily seen that the holomorphic coordinatesz µ′

Ref. [768] has established the bold proposal that exact (all-orders in spin and
coupling) conserved charges of the spinning black hole probe directly arise by
dynamically NJ shifting the conserved quantities of the non-spinning black hole
probe, in the SD sector. By appreciating this idea, we identify the following
exact conserved charges for our Kerr-Newman probe:

The conserved charges in Eq. (11.14) are sufficient to establishintegrability
of the Kerr-Newman probe dynamics in SD Einstein-Maxwell backgrounds ad-
mitting at least two Killing vectors and a Killing-Yano tensor. In Ref. [768],
such integrability has been identified as the property that characterizes black
holes among all possible massive spinning objects. While Ref. [768] has sub-
stantiated this proposal by the Kerr particle, this work reports the same con-
clusion for the Kerr-Newman particle.

In short, we identify superintegrability in the dynamics of the Kerr-Newman
black hole binary system at the zeroth self-force order, yet in the SD sector.

Eq. (11.24) provides the googly formulation of the Kerr-Newman action. To
reiterate, it describes the completely general case of non-SD gravitoelectro-
magnetic field configurations but formulates the SD and ASD modes on an
unequal footing. It provides a worldline action localized on the holomorphic
worldlinez µ′
, the perturbation theory of which manifests the same-helicity
spin exponentiation for incoming positive-helicity photons and gravitons. The
photon coupling is always linear. Graviphoton contact vertices arise iff the
incoming photon carries negative helicity. When examined in the spin length
expansion, the first graviphoton contact vertex appears atO(y 3).
11.9 Interpretation
We end with quick remarks on the physical interpretation by recalling the
comments given in Ref. [769]. Eq. (11.5) represents two charged masses joined
by a Dirac-Misner string (line defect of magnetic and NUT fluxes). Eq. (11.24)
represents a dangling ASD Dirac-Misner string attached to a charged mass.
The shape of the Dirac-Misner string happens to be geodetic for each slice of
simultaneity defined by the worldline proper time. For the Dirac string part,
this is completely justified based on its well-established status as a topological
surface operator.

===== PAGE 307 =====
285
By virtue of the transparent decoding of the NJA revealed by Ref. [164],
we readily realize that this string structure is the precise IR counterpart of
the actual Dirac-Misner string that exists in the UV description of the Kerr-
Newman solution. 1 Namely, it is shown in Ref. [164] that the Kerr-Newman
solution as a holomorphic saddle in Einstein-Maxwell theory represents a pair
of SD and ASD charged Taub-NUT instantons. In conclusion, the holomorphic
worldlinez µ′
is the IR avatar of the worldline of the ASD charged Taub-NUT
instanton that constitutes the Kerr-Newman black hole.
The googly action in Eq. (11.24) perturbs around the SD sector—the sector
in which we, at least, have a full understanding on the dynamics in terms of
remarkable properties such as the superintegrability due to Eqs. (11.14) and
(11.15) or the same-helicity spin exponentiation. The googly agenda means
to approach the complicated dynamics of the Kerr-Newman probe from its
exactly solvable part, which should sound reasonable.
Note that Ref. [226] had speculated on a worldsheet structure, although
no clear physical interpretation nor explicit formulae such as Eqs. (11.5) and
(11.24) were provided.
1Here, IR refers to the point-particle effective theory, while UV refers to GR.

(PQ···Q)Dy7→(PQ···Q)(Q 2dx). See Fig. 11.3 and also Chap. VIII. The
smaller diagram in the upper right corner displays the coefficient of each molecule.

denotedx µ,Dy µ, (Pℓ)µ, and (Qℓ)µν, respectively.

===== PAGE 309 =====

C h a p t e r 12
SPINSPACETIME
We show that Poincar´ e invariance directly implies the existence of a complexi-
fied Minkowski space whose real and imaginary directions unify spacetime and
spin, which we dub spinspacetime. Despite the intrinsic noncommutativity of
spin, spinspacetime exhibits mutually commuting holomorphic coordinates.
Its twistorial construction can derive the NJ shift property of spinning black
holes by massive half-Fourier transforming complexified on-shell kinematics,
which encode a spinning analog of equivalence principle.
This section reproduces the contents of Ref. [672], J.-H. Kim, “Asymptotic spin-
spacetime,”Phys. Rev. D111no. 10, (2025) 105011,arXiv:2309.11886 [hep-th].
12.1 Introduction
In the 70s, Ezra Ted Newman investigated the curious idea of unifying space-
time and spin into a complex geometry [252–259]. While offering a unique
perspective on relativistic angular momenta, it provided a suggestive argu-
ment for the NJ [179, 202] derivation of spinning black hole solutions where
spin turns into an imaginary deviation. However, it remains uncertain if the
NJ shift really follows solely from this complex geometry, since Newman’s
argument implicitly counts on an assumption that the black hole would be
localized in the complex for an unknown reason. On a related note, it has also
remained unclear whether this concept is necessarily limited to black holes or
not. Unfortunately, this line of ideas appears to be no longer actively revived
in the current literature.
In this chapter, we aim to give a dedicated spotlight on this “forgotten
lore” and unveil its relevance to the modern program of describing spinning
black holes in terms of scattering amplitudes [98, 206–212, 214, 218, 219, 226,
673, 795]. As the very first step towards this modern resurrection, we endow
it with a name: “spinspacetime.” We provide a complete definition of spin-
spacetime that unifies various perspectives [252–259, 330, 807, 810, 820–830].
Especially, we highlight its characteristic commutator structure, which, to our
knowledge, was not investigated in Newman’s works [252–259]. Consequently,

===== PAGE 310 =====
288
we refine Newman’s ideas to precise statements. In particular, we envision a
new derivation of the NJ shift from scattering amplitudes, which may trigger
an extension of the on-shell/twistor diagrams [831–837] to massive states.
Our central findings are twofold. Firstly, we show that spinspacetimeuni-
versallyarises in any massive system exhibiting global Poincar´ e symmetry:
particles and fields in asymptotically flat spacetimes, or even systems that
may lack a spacetime formulation. We construct real variablesx µ,y µ directly
from the Poincar´ e charges. Then Poincar´ e symmetry dictates their commuta-
tors to be
[z,z] = 0,[z,¯z]̸= 0,[¯z,¯z] = 0, (12.1)
ifz µ =x µ +iyµ and ¯zµ =x µ−iyµ. These complex coordinates unify spacetime
and spin as real and imaginary parts, which can be seen from the following
split of the total angular momentum into orbital and spin parts:
J= (x∧p) +∗(y∧p) =⇒J + = (z∧p) +.(12.2)
Here, an intrinsic association between chirality and holomorphy is implied,
as the SD part of the total angular momentum,J +, arises solely from the
holomorphic coordinateszas∗→+i. Therefore, a complexified Minkowski
space exhibiting a fascinating geometry arises, whose complex coordinatesz µ,
¯zµ (1) realize a particular “off-diagonal” form of commutators as in Eq. (12.1)
and (2) are inherently linked with chirality as in Eq. (12.2). This provides the
complete characterization of spinspacetime.
Secondly, we specialize this framework in the scattering problem of mas-
sive spinning states from asymptotically free regions. To this end, we adopt
a twistorial implementation of spinspacetime, in which case the off-diagonal
form of Eq. (12.1) originates from the oscillator commutator algebra (K¨ ahler
geometry) [378, 814, 838] of twistor space:
[Z,Z] = 0,[Z, ¯Z]̸= 0,[ ¯Z, ¯Z] = 0.(12.3)
In this description, the NJ shift (as localizations of scattering amplitudes at
complex positions [98, 206–212, 214]) is directly derived from complexified
on-shell kinematics in Fig. 12.2 viamassivehalf-Fourier transforms. As they
provide a massive counterpart of the maximally helicity violating (MHV) kine-
matics [836] and describe a spinning analog of equivalence principle, a novel
perspective is provided for the minimality [98, 206–209] of spinning black holes.

===== PAGE 311 =====
289
In conclusion, spinspacetime is a universal framework for any massive Poincar´ e-
invariant system, in which the minimality of spinning black holes is strikingly
manifest through its distinctive complex-geometrical structures. This refined
understanding fully resolves the questions left by Newman’s works and allows
us to extend and generalize its curious story with exciting future applications.
12.2 Reconstructing Spacetime from Poincar´ e Symmetry
In any system enjoying global Poincar´ e symmetry, there shall exist Poincar´ e
charges:
[Jµν,J ρσ] =i(−4δ [µ
[κην][ρδσ]
λ])Jκλ,
[Jµν,pρ] =ip σ(−2ησ[µδν]
ρ),[p µ,pν] = 0.
(12.4)
Additionally, the notion of mass dimension is accompanied in any physical
situation. This amounts to having an operatorDsuch that
[Jµν,D] = 0,[p µ,D] = +ip µ,(12.5)
assigning dimensions 0 and +1 toJandp, respectively.
Suppose the system is massive so that its configurations do not realizep 2 =
0. Then, define
xµ =J µνpν/p2−Dp µ/p2,(12.6)
as a function ofJ,p, andD. By straightforward calculations, it follows that
xµ exactly transforms like a position vector in Minkowski space under the
Poincar´ e group action: [xµ,J ρσ] =i(−2η µ[ρδσ]ν)xν, [xµ,pρ] =iδ µρ. In this
manner, a Minkowski space emerges from Poincar´ e symmetry.
It should be clear that this construction applies toanymassive system with
global Poincar´ e symmetry: a particle, a group of particles, a field, a group of
fields, or even a system not prescribed in a spacetime formulation. The prime
example is a massive system in an asymptotically flat spacetime, in which
case our argument asserts that aflat bulkemerges from the Poincar´ e charges
at asymptotic infinity.
A few remarks are in order. First, suppose we have only assumed the strict
Poincar´ e algebra in Eq. (12.4). ThenJ µνpν/p2 behaves almost, but not per-
fectly, like a position vector. Amusingly, taking Eq. (12.6) then as an ansatz,
imposing the desired transformation behavior ofx µ mandates the commuta-
tion relations in Eq. (12.5), so the same construction is reproduced. Next, at

===== PAGE 312 =====
290
the technical level, the above calculations will closely parallel those carried
out in Refs. [839, 840]. However, Refs. [839, 840] specifically restricted their
scope to a free particle, whereas our very point here is the universality of this
construction.
12.3 Spin-induced Spacetime Fuzziness
Next, we observe a puzzling feature in this notion of an emergent flat space-
time: it is noncommutative as
[xµ,xν] =− i
p2Sµν,(12.7)
which directly follows from Eqs. (12.4) and (12.6). HereS µν denotes the trans-
verse projection ofJ µν such thatp µSµν = 0. Namely, it is the spin angular
momentum which by definition is the part of the Lorentz chargeJµν unexplain-
able by the orbital angular momentum computed with spacetime coordinates:
Jµν = 2x[µpν] +S µν.
This peculiarity is inevitable in the sense that Eq. (12.6) is the only Poincar´ e-
covariant and real spacetime coordinates that can be directly constructed from
Jµν,p µ, andD, as implied by the analysis provided in the supplemental ma-
terial. Indeed, the noncommutativity in Eq. (12.7) has been observed in a
considerable amount of work [793, 807, 810, 821–830, 839, 841–850] from var-
ious angles and from numerous spinning particle models.
12.4 Unification of Spacetime and Spin
In the co-moving frame of the momentum, Eq. (12.7) boils down to [x i,xj] =
(iℏ/m2c2)εijkSk, which implies a noncommutativity scale of∆x∼s 1/2 (ℏ/mc)
for spins. Here, we have temporarily restored the fundamental constants.
Meanwhile, recall the famous commutator, [S i,Sj] =iℏε ijkSk. Amusingly,
these right-hand sides can be canceled by composing complex combinations
xi∓iS i/mc, precisely because the imaginary unit squares to−1.
Let us implement this solution to the noncommutativity puzzle in a fully
Poincar´ e-covariant way. Define the spin length pseudovector, which describes
the Pauli-Lubanski pseudovector normalized in the units of length:
yµ =−∗J µνpν/p2 =⇒S µν =ε µνρσyrpσ.(12.8)
Then Eq. (12.4) implies thaty µ transforms like a tangent vector: [y µ,J ρσ] =
i(−2ηµ[ρδσ]ν)yν, [yµ,pρ] = 0. In turn, forz µ =x µ +iyµ, Eqs. (12.4) and (12.5)

===== PAGE 313 =====
291
imply
[zµ,zν] = [¯zµ,¯zν] = 0,
[zµ,¯zν] =− 2
p2

2y(µpν) +iε µνρσyρpσ

. (12.9)
Remarkably,z µ themselves arecommutative.
In conclusion, a complexified Minkowski space [611], 1 equipped with the
commutators in Eq. (12.9) featuring commutative holomorphic coordinates,
can be constructed in any massive system with global Poincar´ e symmetry.
This complexified Minkowski space is thespinspacetime.
One might wonder if the use of the imaginary unit, seemingly a bit radical,
is really necessary. However, as we have shown in a bootstrap analysis in
the supplemental material, this isthe only Poincar´ e-covariant solutionto the
noncommutativity puzzle. This uniqueness seems to have not been realized
explicitly, although proposals for the complex-valued coordinates have existed
[820, 821].
Thus, it appears that Poincar´ e invariance intrinsically entails a curious com-
plex geometry, which we then must pay attention to. Especially, it is easily
checked that the Poincar´ e group action preserves the complex structure: it
does not mix up holomorphic and anti-holomorphic variables. Hence there
is a possibility for this complex structure, or holomorphy, of spinspacetime
acquiring a physical significance as a frame-independent notion.
12.5 Newman’s Derivation of Spinspacetime
Indeed, holomorphy in spinspacetime is intrinsically associated withchirality. 2
This point is emphasized in Newman’s original derivation of spinspacetime
[252], sketched in Eq. (12.2). To recapitulate, Newman derives the complex
coordinates by observing that the SD and ASD parts of the angular momentum
areJ ± = ((x±iy)∧p) ±, where (α∧β) ± denotes the SD/ASD projection of
the bivector (α∧β) µν =α µβν−α νβµ. Crucially, the SD partJ + depends
solely on the holomorphic position, while theASD partJ − depends solely on
theanti-holomorphic position. In this light, the imaginary unit inx±iyhas
1Mathematically, this is the spaceC 4 equipped with the flat metricη C whose signature
reduces to Lorentzian on the real sectionz µ = ¯zµ [350]. One may regard it as the tangent
bundle of Minkowski space equipped with the adapted complex structure[743, 776–779].
2The easiest way of seeing this is to realize thaty µ is a pseudovector: parity flip induces
complex conjugation,z µ↔¯zµ.

===== PAGE 314 =====
292
originated from a Hodge (electric-magnetic [830]) duality between orbital and
spin angular momenta, (x∧p) and∗(y∧p).
Newman [252]’s approach to spinspacetime is rather independent from the
approach in the previous sections where the focus is put on the commuta-
tor structure [820, 821]. However, we emphasize that both perspectives cap-
ture essential defining features of spinspacetime: the geometry of spinspace-
time is completely characterized only ifboth(1) the commutator structure in
Eq. (12.1) (more precisely Eq. (12.9)) and (2) the association between holo-
morphy and chirality tracing back to Eq. (12.2) are understood.
These two unique geometrical features lead to remarkable physical implica-
tions. In fact, they can be demonstrated nicely in a twistorial implementation
of spinspacetime, which we now elaborate on.
12.6 Spinspacetime from Massive Twistor Space
The astute reader will realize that the development so far strongly resonates
with the very spirit of twistor theory. First of all, spacetime is regarded
as a secondary construct [378, 380, 814]. And not to mention, a complex-
geometrical structure is disclosed. Further, the [z µ,¯zν] bracket takes a simple
form in the spinor notation, as will be revealed shortly in Eq. (12.12). In ad-
dition,D=−p µxµ in Eq. (12.5) and (12.6) is the dilatation charge. These all
point to an intimate connection to twistor theory where complex geometry,
spinors, and conformal algebra play central roles.
A definition of twistor space is the vector spaceC 4 equipped with a (2,2)-
signature Hermitian form, serving as the linear representation space of U(2,2)
[378, 814]. Geometrically, a twistor represents a null ray in complexified
Minkowski space [378, 611, 814]. To implement the mass, one considers two
copies of twistor space,C 8∼= C4×C 4 [377, 796, 802], where U(2,2) acts from
the left and U(2) acts from the right with a shared U(1). In essence, this
realizes a massive momentum as a sum of two null momenta. This space
C8 is themassive twistor space. According to a modern view developed in
Refs. [673, 794], which differs a bit from the original interpretation taken in
the 70’s twistor particle program [377, 796], the right SU(2) is the massive
little group while the shared U(1) is gauge.
Having defined the massive twistor space, the next step is to construct
its spinspacetime coordinates explicitly. LetZ AI = (λαI,iµ ˙αI) and ¯ZIA =

===== PAGE 315 =====
293
(−i¯µI

twistor space. Here A,B,···are SU(2,2) (Dirac spinor) indices, whileI,J,···
are SU(2) indices. This space is a K¨ ahler vector space [378, 814, 838], so
the commutation relations are given by the oscillator algebra as sketched in
Eq. (12.3): [ZAI, ¯ZJ B] =δ ABδJI, to be explicit.
The U(2,2) generators are given byG AB =Z AI ¯ZIA, the Weyl block decom-
position of which gives

with any other generators and induces a central extension from SU(2,2) to

secondary constructs emerging from intersections between null rays.
In conclusion, the spinspacetime of massive twistor space is simply the com-
plexified Minkowski space which its incidence relation in Eq. (12.13) defines. 4
Intriguingly, this implies that the noncommutativity in Eq. (12.7) could be
reinterpreted as the fuzziness which spacetime points acquires in twistor the-
ory as light rays become fundamental objects [378, 380, 807, 810, 814, 822–829]:
see Fig. 12.1.
The twistorial picture associates fascinating geometric origins to the defin-
ing features of spinspacetime. First, the off-diagonal commutators (Eq. (12.1))
have signaled the K¨ ahler geometry of twistor space (Eq. (12.3)). Second, holo-
morphy in spinspacetime corresponds to the holomorphy of twistors. Third,
the link between holomorphy and chirality in spinspacetime (Eq. (12.2)) traces
back to that of twistor space. Note that this link is deeply endemic in twistor
space: theholomorphicZ AI contains theleft-handedspinor-helicity variable
λαI.
12.7 S-Matrix in Massive Twistor Space
Eventually, we study scattering in asymptotically flat spacetimes. This setup
concerns the spinspacetime which Poincar´ e charges in asymptotically free re-
gions reconstruct, together with an asymptotic massive twistor space as a
phase space.

sive spinor-helicity variablesλ αI, ˜λI˙α [206]. The massive twistor space can be

12.8 What Defines Spinning Black Holes
Now we ask the following question: What is the simplest S-matrix in massive
twistor space? For the case of massive-massive-massless three-particle scatter-
ing with equal masses, the on-shell kinematics exhibit twoextremalcases that
freeze either of the left-handed or right-handed massive spinor-helicity vari-
ables. Let us call thesezigandzag 5 kinematics and denote them respectively
as white and black blobs as shown in Fig. 12.2, analogously to the massless
on-shell diagram notation in Refs. [831–837]. Then momentum conservation
fully refines their delta function supports as

Furthermore, we stipulate a link between chirality and holomorphy: thezig
andzagkinematics are realized forpositiveandnegativehelicities of the mass-
less leg, respectively. With this postulate, the zig and zag kinematics embody
the scattering amplitudes statement of an analog of equivalence principle in
half-flat backgrounds, which we dubspinning equivalence principle.
Suppose a spinning object traveling in a SD background spacetime. What
would be its simplest motion? By definition, the left-handed spinor bundle
is flat, which one can locally trivialize. If the gravitational forces are to arise
uniquely from this geometry, the object’s left-handed Lorentz frame would
remain constant in local laboratories. This deduces the spinning equivalence
principle: the left-handed (right-handed) frame of a spinning object is covari-
antly constant under dynamical evolution in SD (ASD) backgrounds.
Of course, in a modern understanding, the equivalence principle is rather a
statement about the minimality of a test object, which can be broken by non-
minimal couplings. For example, a scalar particle endowed with nonzero non-
minimal Wilson coefficients will not follow the geodesic trajectory as desired by
the equivalence principle. Similarly, the spinning equivalence principle rather
6Cf. Penrose’s argument in Refs. [312, 313] that the notion of a “single-graviton space-
time” is inherently complex.

===== PAGE 319 =====
297
proposes the most ideal behavior which a spinning object may exhibit.
Remarkably, we find that such an ideal behavior is exhibited byblack holes.
To see this, consider the massive half-Fourier transform of the zig kinematics
to the twistorcoherent statebasis 7:

In sum, spinning black holes are characterized by the extremal complex-
ified on-shell kinematics in Fig. 12.2, which encode the spinning equivalence
principle. Notably, we could have also assumed any multiplicity of massless
quanta, building up arbitrary half-flat backgrounds. This derives the same-
helicity spin exponentiation [210–212] at all multiplicities via the half-Fourier
transform. Eventually, this case would arise from a gluing of the same-helicity
on-shell diagrams. It will be interesting if a smart gluing is viable for mixed
helicities as well, given the spurious pole problem [206, 209, 214].
A few remarks are in order. Firstly, in the above derivation of NJ shift,
the off-diagonal commutator structure of the massive twistor space (as the
structure of half-Fourier transforms), namely the K¨ ahler geometry, has played
an important role. To make this point more evident, consider the following
identity:

Another essential role is played by the intrinsic association between chi-
rality and holomorphy. To see this, suppose we knew that the object is a
spinning black hole from the beginning. While sitting at the asymptotic in-
finity with an experimentalist mindset, we “measure” the S-matrix to deduce
what background this black hole had interacted with. If the S-matrix isholo-
morphic, then, by the off-diagonal structure, theanti-holomorphicvariable ˜λ
solely exhibits precession. But crucially, theanti-holomorphicspinor-helicity
variable isright-handed. Due to this very endemic link between holomorphy
and handedness, the background must have been filled withright-handed, i.e.,
SD fields.
On a related note, the exponent in Eq. (12.19) describes the SD angular
momentumJ + = (z∧p) + times a factor, providing a twistorial realization of
the equations employed in Ref. [207].
For generic massive spinning objects, the kinematics is not extremal. For ex-
ample,λand ˜λare shifted symmetrically (cf. Ref. [98]) when the object’s spin-
induced multipole moments [97] vanish from the quadrupole order [673]. The
complexified kinematics we have studied is sensitive to all multipole moments,
while Newman’s explorations [252–259] focused only on the dipole coupling
(gyromagnetic ratio).
Lastly, we were able to deduce the essential physics just by focusing on the
delta function support, similarly as in Ref. [836]. A full-blown theory should
not only incorporate themx h prefactors [206] but also properly formulate the
independent massive little group orbits. The first-quantized massive twistor
particle [673, 794] could provide a reference, which concretely reproduces the
delta function supports considered above [673].
12.9 Conclusions
In this work, we established the notion of spinspacetime as a universal conse-
quence of Poincar´ e symmetry. Its characteristic geometric structures, which
trace back to those of twistor space, revealed that spinning black holes obey
a spinning analog of equivalence principle. The Newman-Janis shift factors
are effortlessly obtained by massive half-Fourier transforming the extremal

===== PAGE 321 =====
299
on-shell kinematics in Fig. 12.2.
This revival of a curious concept should trigger various future directions.
Firstly, the theory of bulk spinspacetime will be developed, paralleling the his-
torical development of curved twistor theory [853]. To this end, one expects
a curved-spacetime generalization or reincarnation of Newman and Winicour
[252]’s Hodge duality between orbital and spin angular momenta in Eq. (12.2).9
Secondly, applications to PM gravity will be pursued. Lastly, the massive on-
shell diagrams and their gluing should be investigated, reminiscently of the
massless story [349, 831–837]. It will be exciting if a complete on-shell frame-
work, incorporating both massless and massive legs, can serve as a powerful
tool for both theoretical and practical directions.
9Note that we have realized this general-relativistic “orbit-spin duality” in Chaps. IX and
X: the local orbital angular momentum due to geodesic deviation turns into a spin angular
momentum, giving rise to the general-relativistic holomorphic spinspacetime coordinates in
the SD sector. In fact, we had developed and established the complete mathematical and
physical theory of curved spinspacetime from this perspective of orbit-spin duality, which,
unfortunately, had to be omitted from this thesis due to lack of space.

We demonstrate how the framework of spinspacetime facilitates a systematic
derivation of all-orders-in-spin EoM of spinning black holes in their point-
particle effective theory. A model-independent derivation of BMT equations
and their all-orders-in-spin extensions is demonstrated. A model-independent

of the non-spinning probe. A maximally superintegrable subsector is identified

13.1.3 Symplectic Leaves
Lastly, let us comprehend the above results from a mathematical perspective.
A Poisson manifold is a manifold equipped with a Poisson bracket{f,g}=
Π(df,dg) between functionsfandg, which arises from a bivector Π. A sym-
plectic manifold is a manifold equipped with a symplectic formω, which is a
closed nondegenerate two-form. Symplectic manifolds are Poisson manifolds,
but the converse is not true. This is because the Poisson bivector Π can be
degenerate, unlike the symplectic formω.
Yet, any Poisson manifold is foliated bysymplectic leaves, which are (con-
nected) symplectic submanifolds of maximal dimension. The dimension of
symplectic leaves is determined by the rank of the Poisson bivector Π.

===== PAGE 325 =====
303
Provided that ˆyµ is nontrivial, ˆxµ, ˆyµ, andp µ in Eq. (13.1) together describe
a 10-dimensional Poisson submanifold embedded in the phase spaceP. Its
symplectic leaves are 8-dimensional. To see this, regard the Poisson brackets
in Eq. (13.1) as components of an antisymmetric matrix and compute its rank:
8. Furthermore, examining its kernel shows that each of these symplectic
leaves is the locus of
−p2 =m 2,−p 2 ˆy2 =w 2,(13.8)
wheremandware constants. Physically, the first equation computes therest
massmwhereas the second equation computes thespin magnitudew.
In this way, it follows that one can always identify an 8-dimensional sym-
plectic submanifoldP 8(m,w) inside any phase spacePof a massive system
with nontrivial ˆyµ. This symplectic submanifold will be referred to as the8-
dimensional universal phase spaceof the massive spinning system, on which
the rest mass and the spin magnitude are fixed to constant valuesmandw:
P⊃P 8(m,w).(13.9)
In turn, 8 sets the minimal bound for the dimension of a massive spinning
phase space. It achieves three translational degrees of freedom and one spin-
ning degree of freedom. It can be seen thatP 8(m,w) is homeomorphic to
R6×S 2, which describes the coadjoint orbit [815, 855, 856] of the Poincar´ e
group [815, 840, 857, 858]. It follows thatP 8(m,w) is equivalent to Souriau’s
“8-dimensional space of motion” reviewed in Ref. [859].
Similarly,x µ, ˆyµ, andp µ in Eq. (13.5) together describe an 11-dimensional
Poisson submanifold inPwhose symplectic leaves are 10-dimensional. Com-
putation shows that each symplectic leaf is the locus of−p 2 ˆy2 =w 2. This
identifies the10-dimensional universal phase spaceP 10(w), on which the spin
magnitude is fixed to a constant valuew:
P⊃P 10(w).(13.10)
It can be seen thatP 10(w) is homeomorphic toR 8×S 2.
It is possible to reproduce the 8-dimensional universal phase space in Eq. (13.9)
from the 10-dimensional universal phase space in Eq. (13.10):

Mathematically, quotients of the form Eq. (13.11) could be calledsymplectic

reduction always reduces the dimension by an even number.
The physicists’ toolkit for symplectic reduction is known asDirac bracket
[862–864]. In this work, we use Dirac brackets to explicitly realize symplectic
quotients as symplectic submanifolds.

Surely, this reduction recipe applies universally in every massive spinning sys-
tem.

13.2.1 Couplings as Symplectic Perturbations
Our point of departure is the celebrated fact in symplectic geometry that a
manifestly gauge-invariant coupling of particles to electromagnetic fields is vi-
able by modifying the symplectic structure in terms of the field strength. This
construction is due to Souriau [815] and also traces back to Feynman [816].
Historically, this manifestly gauge-invariant method has served as an impor-
tant milestone in the study of particle mechanics in the symplectic framework
[865–869]. Unfortunately, it appears to be less recognized in recent literature,
so we shall provide a brief review (see also Ref. [764]).
For the sake of concreteness, let us suppose a simple example: a massive
scalar particle in Minkowski space. This is a Hamiltonian system defined on
the cotangent bundleT ∗M. In the free theory, this phase space is equipped
with the symplectic form and Hamiltonian
ω◦ =dp µ∧dxµ, ϕ 0 = 1
2m

p2 +m 2

.(13.15)
We have started to put the accent ◦ to explicitly indicate free-theory objects.
The canonical Poisson bracket reads
{xµ,xν}◦ = 0,{x µ,pν}◦ =δ µ
ν,{p µ,pν}◦ = 0.(13.16)
The Souriau-Feynman method of electromagnetic coupling modifies the ge-
ometric data in Eq. (13.15) as
ω=dp µ∧dxµ +qF, ϕ 0 = 1
2m

p2 +m 2

